### Error Schema for Identifying Premature Task Completion Errors in Multi-Agent Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Distinctive Patterns or Signals:**
  - The agent immediately selects a final step or action without considering the preliminary steps necessary for the task.
  - The agent provides a detailed rationale that justifies the selection of a step that logically should not be the first action.
  - The agent's response includes a decision labeled as "TASK_COMPLETE" when the task clearly requires a sequence of actions or further steps.

- **Telltale Signs in Agent's Behavior or Responses:**
  - The agent skips over essential preparatory actions or information gathering steps.
  - The agent's decision is based on assumptions or hypothetical scenarios rather than a clear understanding of the task requirements.
  - The agent's rationale introduces new steps or actions that are not part of the given options but are necessary for the task, suggesting a misunderstanding of the options.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Typical Contextual Conditions:**
  - The task involves a process or sequence of steps that need to be followed in a specific order.
  - The task is part of a scientific investigation or experiment where initial observations or data collection is crucial.
  - The task requires the agent to choose the first step from a list of options, some of which are more logically suited to later stages of the process.

- **Preceding Sequence of Interactions:**
  - The user presents a clear, multi-step task with a specific question about the initial step.
  - The agent does not request clarification or additional information about the task.
  - The agent does not engage in a systematic breakdown of the task to identify the logical sequence of steps.
  - The agent does not consult other specialists or capabilities that could provide additional context or information.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions to Determine Error Presence:**
  - "Is there a step that should logically precede the one you have chosen?"
  - "Can you explain how the selected step fits into the overall sequence of the investigation?"
  - "Have you considered the need for any preparatory actions before committing to this step?"

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Step Sequence Validity:** Check if the chosen step can logically be the first in the sequence of actions required for the task.
  - **Task Requirements Compliance:** Ensure that the agent's decision aligns with the specific requirements and constraints of the task as presented.
  - **Premature Justification:** Analyze if the rationale provided introduces steps or actions that should be part of the task but are not listed as options, indicating a potential misunderstanding of the task.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - "First, we should..." followed by a step that is typically done later in the process.
  - "Before we..." or "After we..." used to justify why a step is chosen, but the step itself is not the first in the logical sequence.
  - "This minimizes bias" or "This ensures consistency" when the chosen step is not a preparatory step.
  - "Pilot" or "initial observations" mentioned as part of the rationale for choosing a step that is not listed as the first option.
  - "TASK_COMPLETE" used in the response when the task is not yet complete or when the question specifically asks for the first step.

This template is designed to help identify when an agent prematurely concludes a task by selecting a step that logically should not be the first, without adequately breaking down the task or gathering necessary information.

### Error Schema for Identifying Similar Errors in Future Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Misinterpretation of Reaction Type and Energy Flow:**
  - The agent incorrectly identifies the nature of the reaction (endothermic vs. exothermic) based on a misunderstanding of the energy flow in the system.
  - The agent confuses the direction of energy transfer, particularly in phase changes like freezing.

- **Incorrect Application of Thermodynamic Principles:**
  - The agent misapplies thermodynamic principles, such as the heat of fusion, to conclude the reaction type.
  - The agent incorrectly equates the freezing of water droplets with the absorption of energy by the reaction.

- **Logical Flaws in Reasoning:**
  - The agent uses flawed logic to deduce the reaction type, such as assuming that the freezing of water droplets must be endothermic.
  - The agent fails to recognize that freezing is an exothermic process, releasing heat to the surroundings.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Contextual Conditions:**
  - The question involves a scenario where a chemical reaction is observed to have an effect on the environment (e.g., freezing of water droplets).
  - The scenario includes a phase change (freezing, boiling, etc.) as a key observation.

- **Sequence of Interactions:**
  - The agent is asked to determine the nature of a chemical reaction based on an observed environmental effect.
  - The agent makes a quick decision without breaking down the task into smaller, manageable steps or verifying the thermodynamic principles involved.
  - The agent may reference thermodynamic data (like the heat of fusion) but misinterpret its implications in the context of the reaction.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions:**
  - "What is the direction of heat transfer in the freezing process?"
  - "How does the freezing of water droplets on the beaker relate to the energy changes in the chemical reaction?"
  - "Can you explain the difference between endothermic and exothermic reactions and how they affect the surroundings?"

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Step-by-Step Breakdown:** Check if the agent has broken down the task into steps, specifically identifying the direction of heat flow and the nature of the observed phase change.
  - **Thermodynamic Verification:** Ensure the agent correctly applies thermodynamic principles, such as recognizing that freezing releases heat.
  - **Logical Consistency:** Review the agent's reasoning to ensure that the logical flow from the observation to the conclusion is consistent and accurate.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - "The water droplets freeze, so the reaction must be endothermic."
  - "Freezing is an endothermic process, which means the reaction is endothermic."
  - "Since the reaction causes the water to freeze, it absorbs energy."
  - "The freezing indicates that the reaction is absorbing heat from the surroundings."
  - "The droplets freeze, suggesting that the reaction is taking in heat."

### Template Usage:
- **When a question involves a chemical reaction and an environmental observation (like phase changes),** look for the agent's discussion of heat transfer and reaction types.
- **If the agent mentions freezing or other phase changes** without correctly associating them with exothermic processes, this could be a red flag.
- **Ask follow-up questions** to probe the agent's understanding of the direction of heat flow and the implications for the reaction type.
- **Review the logical flow** of the agent's response to ensure that the conclusion follows from a correct application of thermodynamic principles and observations.

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Premature Task Completion:** The agent declares the task complete (`TASK_COMPLETE`) without engaging in any systematic information gathering or breaking down the task into steps.
- **Overly Confident Assertions:** The agent provides a detailed explanation of the chosen answer, which includes specific numerical data or scientific perspectives, without any evidence of having used the available capabilities (WebSurfer, FileSurfer, Coder, CodeExecutor) to verify or gather this information.
- **Lack of Initial Exploration:** The agent skips the step of analyzing or breaking down the task, moving directly to a conclusion based on internal knowledge or assumptions.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Contextual Conditions:**
  - The task involves a complex question that requires specific scientific knowledge or data to answer correctly.
  - The question has multiple plausible answers, each of which could be argued based on different aspects of the topic.
  - The task is presented in a format that suggests the need for research or analysis to select the best answer.
- **Preceding Interactions:**
  - The conversation begins with the presentation of a question and the available answer options.
  - The Planner is asked to decide the first action, which should involve planning steps for research or analysis.
  - There is no prior result or information provided for the Planner to build upon, indicating the need for an initial information-gathering step.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions:**
  - "Did you use any of the available capabilities to gather information before making your decision?"
  - "Can you explain how you arrived at this conclusion without performing any research or analysis?"
  - "What steps did you take to ensure that your answer is the best among the given options?"
- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Step Verification:** Check if the agent has followed the steps of breaking down the task, gathering information systematically, and building upon previous results.
  - **Capability Utilization:** Verify if the agent has used any of the provided capabilities (WebSurfer, FileSurfer, Coder, CodeExecutor) to support their answer.
  - **Evidence of Research:** Look for any references to sources, data, or methods used to arrive at the conclusion.
- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - "Based on my understanding," followed by a detailed scientific explanation without mentioning the use of any capabilities.
  - "The best single choice is..." without prior steps or evidence of research.
  - "TASK_COMPLETE" declared at the first step of the conversation.
  - "Remember to: 1. Break down the task into manageable steps 2. Gather information systematically 3. Build upon previous results" is acknowledged but not acted upon.

### Template Application:
- **When to Apply:**
  - Use this template when the Planner is asked to make an initial decision on a complex or scientific question.
  - Apply if the Planner quickly concludes the task without engaging in any of the systematic steps outlined in the task instructions.
- **How to Apply:**
  - Review the Planner's response for the presence of error signatures.
  - Analyze the context to see if the conditions that typically lead to this error are present.
  - Use the detection heuristics to ask follow-up questions and assess the Planner's methodology.

### Example Usage:
- **Conversation Review:**
  - **Question:** "Which BEST describes the surface of the Earth over billions of years?"
  - **Planner's Response:** "The best single choice is that high mountains gradually wear down until most of the Earth is at sea level (Option B), acknowledging uplift exists but is not globally balanced at the same rate and scale. TASK_COMPLETE"
  - **Application of Template:**
    - **Error Signature:** Premature task completion, overly confident assertion with detailed scientific explanation.
    - **Contextual Analysis:** The task involves a complex scientific question with multiple plausible answers and no prior results.
    - **Detection Heuristic:** Ask, "Did you use any of the available capabilities to gather information before making your decision?" and "What steps did you take to ensure that your answer is the best among the given options?"

=== Schema for Error Log 37 ===
Generated Schema:
### Error Schema for Error Identification: Misinterpretation of Technical Terms

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Misaligned Terminology:** The agent uses a term in a different context or meaning than intended by the question, leading to a misinterpretation of the correct answer.
- **Premature Conclusion:** The agent reaches a conclusion based on initial or partial information without verifying or considering all the details.
- **Overgeneralization:** The agent applies a general understanding to a specific scenario, potentially overlooking unique characteristics or conditions.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Context of Technical Terms:** The error occurs in a conversation where technical terms (e.g., "burns") have multiple meanings or contexts (e.g., surface temperature vs. core temperature).
- **Lack of Domain-Specific Verification:** The agent relies on general knowledge or a quick search without engaging in a detailed, domain-specific verification process.
- **Misinterpretation of Question Intent:** The agent misinterprets the question's intent, focusing on a less relevant aspect of the topic (e.g., surface temperature instead of core conditions).

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Clarification Questions:**
  - "What specific context are you using for the term [technical term]?"
  - "Can you explain how [technical term] is relevant to the [specific scenario]?"
  - "Are there different interpretations of [technical term] that could affect the answer?"
- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Term Context Analysis:** Identify the context in which the technical term is used in the question and compare it with the context in which the agent uses it.
  - **Option Relevance Check:** Evaluate each option to determine its relevance to the specific characteristics or conditions mentioned in the question.
  - **Cross-Verification:** Use multiple reliable sources to cross-verify the agent's interpretation and conclusions, especially when dealing with technical or scientific concepts.
- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - "Based on general [field] knowledge, the best answer appears to be..."
  - "The term [technical term] refers to [different context] rather than [intended context]."
  - "Among the provided choices, the distinguishing feature should interpret [technical term] as..."
  - "Critically, the [specific phase] features [unique characteristic] with [specific condition] compared to the [other phase] state."

### Example Application:
**Question:** A red giant star differs from main sequence stars because it A) burns at a cooler temperature., B) is always larger than other suns., C) has greater density and gravity., D) fuses hydrogen to make helium.

**Agent Response:** "The best answer among the given options appears to be A) burns at a cooler temperature, since red giants have cooler (red) surfaces compared to many main-sequence stars..."

**Detection Using Template:**
- **Error Signatures:** The agent uses "burns" in the context of surface temperature, which is a different interpretation from the nuclear burning conditions implied by the question.
- **Error Context Analysis:** The agent has a general understanding of red giants being cooler but fails to verify the specific context of the term "burns" as it relates to core conditions.
- **Detection Heuristics:**
  - **Clarification Question:** "What specific context are you using for the term 'burns'?"
  - **Analytical Framework:** 
    - **Term Context Analysis:** The term "burns" in the question likely refers to the core temperature or fusion process, not the surface temperature.
    - **Option Relevance Check:** Option A is about surface temperature, while the question might be asking about core conditions.
    - **Cross-Verification:** Verify the interpretation of "burns" and the relevance of each option using reliable sources like Britannica, NASA, or academic texts.

=== Schema for Error Log 41 ===
Generated Schema:
### Error Schema for Identifying Knowledge Misapplication Errors in Multi-Agent Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Incorrect Final Answer Despite Correct Intermediate Reasoning:** The agent provides a final answer that contradicts the reasoning or analysis given in the steps leading up to the decision. For example, the agent correctly identifies that light is a form of electromagnetic radiation but concludes with an incorrect option (mechanical energy).
- **Confidence in Incorrect Answer:** The agent expresses a high level of confidence in the final answer, even though the answer is incorrect. This can be seen through declarative statements or the use of strong affirmatives like "definitely" or "certainly."
- **Premature Task Completion:** The agent declares the task as complete and provides a final answer without engaging in further verification or consultation, despite the availability of tools or capabilities that could be used to confirm the correctness of the answer.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Straightforward Knowledge Question:** The task is a direct question that requires specific factual knowledge, often with a clear set of options provided (e.g., multiple-choice questions).
- **Lack of Information Gathering:** The agent does not utilize available tools or capabilities (like WebSurfer, FileSurfer, etc.) to gather additional information or verify the answer, relying solely on internal knowledge.
- **Task-Specific Context Ignored:** The agent fails to consider the specific context or requirements of the task, leading to a decision that is logically consistent with the internal reasoning but incorrect in the context of the question.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Consistency Check:** Ask, "Is the final answer consistent with the reasoning provided in the analysis?" This helps to identify discrepancies between the agent's intermediate steps and the final conclusion.
- **Tool Utilization Review:** Question, "Did the agent use any available tools to gather or verify information before declaring the task complete?" This can highlight whether the agent bypassed potential resources that could have corrected the error.
- **Contextual Relevance:** Inquire, "Does the final answer align with the specific context of the question?" For example, in a question about energy transformation in a light bulb, check if the answer is relevant to the physics of light emission.
- **Analytical Framework:** Use a framework that involves breaking down the task into:
  - **Understanding the Question:** What is being asked?
  - **Identifying Key Concepts:** What are the critical elements (e.g., energy types, transformation processes) in the question?
  - **Matching Concepts with Options:** How do the identified concepts align with the provided options?
  - **Verification of Answer:** What steps were taken to ensure the accuracy of the final answer?
- **Key Phrases and Patterns:**
  - **Final Answer Contradiction:** Look for phrases like "The correct answer is X, but I choose Y."
  - **Premature Completion:** Identify patterns where the agent quickly declares "TASK_COMPLETE" without a thorough analysis or use of tools.
  - **Overconfidence:** Phrases such as "I am certain the answer is X" or "The answer is definitely X" when the answer is incorrect.
  - **Ignoring Context:** Statements that show a lack of consideration for the specific details of the task, such as "Generally, it is X" or "Mostly, it is X" without addressing the particular scenario or question.

### Example Application of the Template:
- **User Question:** "What is the primary form of energy produced by a solar panel when it converts sunlight into electricity?"
- **Agent Response:** "Solar panels convert sunlight into thermal energy, which is then used to generate electricity. TASK_COMPLETE. Final Answer: B) thermal."
- **Error Identification Using the Template:**
  - **Consistency Check:** Is the final answer (thermal energy) consistent with the reasoning (solar panels convert sunlight into thermal energy, then generate electricity)?
  - **Tool Utilization Review:** Did the agent use WebSurfer or FileSurfer to verify the process of energy conversion in solar panels?
  - **Contextual Relevance:** Does the answer align with the physics of solar panel operation, which primarily involves converting sunlight directly into electrical energy?
  - **Key Phrases and Patterns:** Does the agent's response show overconfidence or ignore the specific context of the question (e.g., direct conversion of sunlight to electricity)?

This template can be used to systematically analyze future conversations for similar errors where an agent might misapply knowledge or overlook critical details.

### Error Schema for Identifying Similar Errors in Future Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:

  - **Overemphasis on Snippets:** The agent focuses on snippets of text visible in the search results rather than opening and summarizing the full content of the sources.
  - **Inconsistent Progress:** The agent shows a lack of progress in the conversation, repeatedly searching for the same information without advancing the analysis.
  - **Metadata Over Content:** The agent provides detailed metadata (e.g., meta tags, viewport percentages) instead of the requested content and examples.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Typical Contextual Conditions:**
  - **Complex Task Breakdown:** The task involves multiple steps, including identifying core concepts, assessing choices, and confirming with authoritative sources.
  - **Specific Information Requirement:** The task requires the agent to gather and synthesize specific information from diverse, authoritative sources to validate a hypothesis.
  - **Need for Concrete Examples:** The task involves finding concrete examples (e.g., Colorado Plateau) to support the hypothesis.

- **Sequence of Interactions Preceding the Error:**
  - **Initial Hypothesis Formation:** The user forms a working hypothesis based on prior knowledge.
  - **Request for Validation:** The user asks the agent to validate the hypothesis using authoritative sources.
  - **Multiple Searches with Limited Results:** The agent performs multiple searches but fails to extract and synthesize the necessary content, instead providing metadata and snippets repeatedly.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions to Determine Error Presence:**
  - **Content Extraction:** "Has the agent opened and summarized the full content of the sources, or are they only providing snippets and metadata?"
  - **Source Diversity:** "Is the agent using multiple, diverse sources to validate the hypothesis, or are they repeatedly using the same source?"
  - **Hypothesis Support:** "Does the extracted information explicitly support the hypothesis with clear examples and mechanisms, or is it vague and incomplete?"

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Step-by-Step Verification:** Check if the agent has completed each step of the task as requested:
    - **Identify Core Concept:** Is the core concept clearly identified and understood?
    - **Assess Each Choice:** Are all choices assessed using prior knowledge?
    - **Working Hypothesis:** Is a working hypothesis formed and clearly stated?
    - **Information Gap:** Is the information gap explicitly identified and addressed?
    - **Source Gathering:** Are multiple, authoritative sources being gathered and used?
    - **Content Synthesis:** Is the content from these sources being synthesized to support or adjust the hypothesis?
  - **Content Quality:** Evaluate the quality of the information provided:
    - **Relevance:** Is the information directly relevant to the task and hypothesis?
    - **Completeness:** Does the information cover all required aspects (uplift, erosion, examples)?
    - **Clarity:** Is the information presented clearly and concisely?

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - **Repetition of Searches:** "I typed 'Britannica plateau uplift erosion' into the browser search bar."
  - **Metadata Focus:** "The following metadata was extracted from the webpage:"
  - **Partial Information:** "The viewport shows 36% of the webpage, and is positioned at the top of the page."
  - **Lack of Explicit Content:** "The essential criteria for plateaus are low relative relief and some altitude."
  - **Overemphasis on Snippets:** "A dissected plateau forms as a result of upward movement in the Earth's crust."

### Template Summary:
- **Error Signatures:** Look for repeated use of the same source, lack of explicit content extraction, and partial information.
- **Error Context Analysis:** The error typically occurs in a complex task breakdown, where specific information and concrete examples are required, and the agent fails to advance beyond initial searches.
- **Detection Heuristics:** Ask if the agent has opened and summarized full content, used multiple diverse sources, and provided clear, relevant, and complete information. Use a step-by-step verification framework and identify key phrases that indicate metadata focus or partial information.

### Error Schema for Identifying Similar Errors in Future Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:

  - **Lack of Prior Information:** The task is presented without any prior specialist output or context, which might have provided useful background or details.
  - **Multiple Choice Format:** The task is in a multiple-choice format, which can sometimes lead to over-reliance on elimination based on general knowledge rather than a detailed analysis of each option.

  - **Did the agent consider the specific definitions of adaptive characteristics and performance traits?**

  - **Evidence Presentation:** Evaluate if the agent has presented any evidence or cited sources to support the reasoning behind the final answer.
  - **Definition Clarity:** Verify if the agent has a clear understanding of the definitions of key terms in the question, such as "adaptive characteristics" and "performance traits."

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - **"No prior specialist output to summarize."** This phrase indicates that the agent did not seek additional information.
  - **"Therefore, the correct choice is..."** followed immediately by an answer without detailed analysis or evidence.
  - **"Please analyze this task and decide which specialist to engage first."** The agent's response should not be a final answer but rather a plan for engagement.
  - **"Goal: Identify which option is NOT an adaptive characteristic..."** but lacks a systematic approach to evaluating each option.
  - **"TASK_COMPLETE"** without prior steps or engagement with specialists, suggesting a hasty conclusion.

This template helps identify errors where the agent prematurely concludes a task without a thorough analysis or engagement with available resources,

  - The task requires an understanding of basic ecological principles without the need for external data or complex reasoning.

  - The agent immediately begins to analyze the problem without a preliminary step of understanding the given options.

  - The agent does not systematically break down the task into smaller, manageable steps but instead jumps to a detailed conclusion.

  - "However, this scenario also involves..."
  - "In addition to the effects mentioned, we should consider..."
  - "The correct answer is more complex because..."
  - "If we also take into account..."
  - "This leads to a different conclusion that..."

By recognizing these patterns, signals, and contextual conditions, similar errors in future conversations can be identified early, ensuring that the analysis stays aligned with the given task and options.

  - **Initial Correct Analysis:** The agent correctly identifies the primary resources (food and nesting areas) in the initial analysis.
  - **Targeted Searches Without Evidence:** The agent performs targeted searches but does not extract the necessary evidence to confirm the primary resources.
  - **Shift in Understanding:** The agent's understanding shifts towards less common resources (e.g., oxygen and carbon dioxide) due to the lack of direct evidence.
  - **Final Incorrect Confirmation:** The agent confirms the incorrect answer (B) oxygen and carbon dioxide) based on the nuanced or incomplete evidence gathered.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions:**
  - **"What specific resources did the sources mention as primary contested resources for terrestrial vertebrates?"**
  - **"Are the sources you found directly supporting the claim that food and nesting areas are the primary contested resources?"**
  - **"Did you find any sources that explicitly state the two most contested resources in terrestrial biomes?"**
  - **"Why did you choose the resources you identified as the primary contested ones?"**

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Check for Direct Evidence:** Ensure that the agent provides direct quotes or precise paraphrases from reputable sources that explicitly support the correct answer.
  - **Evaluate Source Relevance:** Assess whether the sources found are directly relevant to the question and whether they are from credible, authoritative outlets (e.g., Britannica, university resources, peer-reviewed journals).
  - **Track Shifts in Understanding:** Monitor if the agent's understanding shifts away from the initial correct resources due to a lack of evidence or misinterpretation of sources.
  - **Cross-Reference with Ground Truth:** Verify that the agent's final understanding and decision align with the ground truth or widely accepted ecological knowledge.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - **"No explicit quotes yet confirm"** or **"No direct evidence found"**
  - **"Despite multiple searches"** or **"After several refinements"**
  - **"These two resources are repeatedly highlighted"** or **"The sources consistently mention"**
  - **"Shift in understanding"** or **"New interpretation based on"**
  - **"Ignoring specific terms"** or **"Not emphasizing"**
  - **"Final confirmation with incorrect answer"** or **"Confirming the answer is"**

This template can be used to quickly identify when an agent is struggling to gather direct evidence for a well-known answer and may be misinterpreting or overemphasizing less relevant information.

#### 1. Error Signatures:

- **Premature Task Completion:** The agent declares the task complete and provides a final answer without engaging the appropriate specialists or conducting necessary analyses.
- **Overlooking Relevant Options:** The agent fails to consider or adequately evaluate all provided options, focusing on one that seems plausible but is not necessarily correct.
- **Incorrect Assumptions:** The agent makes assumptions about the scenario that are not supported by the given context or are based on a misunderstanding of the physical environment.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Initial Assessment Without Data:** The error occurs in the first response, where the agent attempts to provide an answer without any prior data or specialist consultation.
- **Lack of Step-by-Step Breakdown:** The task is not broken down into smaller, manageable steps that could help systematically evaluate each option.
- **Insufficient Use of Available Capabilities:** The agent does not utilize available capabilities (WebSurfer, FileSurfer, Coder, CodeExecutor) to gather or verify information, relying instead on its own internal reasoning.
- **Rushed Decision-Making:** The agent quickly decides on an answer without exploring all possible avenues or seeking additional input, suggesting a lack of thoroughness.

  - **Assess Completeness of Analysis:** Determine if the agent has considered all options and their potential impacts, or if it has overlooked any critical elements.
  - **Verify Use of Resources:** Ensure that the agent has utilized the available capabilities to gather necessary information or perform calculations that support its conclusions.
  - **Identify Premature Conclusions:** Look for instances where the agent declares the task complete without a thorough step-by-step analysis or without engaging other specialists.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - **"This is the initial assessment step."** followed by a definitive answer.
  - **"Therefore, the correct option is..."** without prior discussion or data gathering.
  - **"No previous specialist results exist."** leading directly to a final answer.
  - **Lack of Engagement with Specialists:** The agent does not mention or plan to use WebSurfer, FileSurfer, Coder, or CodeExecutor.
  - **Overconfidence in Own Knowledge:** The agent provides detailed calculations or explanations without verifying them through external resources or calculations.

- **Look for Omissions:** Ensure that the Planner has not overlooked any of the options or the interactions between different forces.
- **Verify Resource Use:** Confirm that the Planner has a plan or has already engaged with the appropriate specialists to gather or verify information.
- **Watch for Premature Completion:** If the Planner declares the task complete too early, without a detailed breakdown or verification, it might be an error.

=== Schema for Error Log 58 ===
Generated Schema:
### Error Schema for Identifying Similar Errors in Future Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures
- **Distinctive Patterns or Signals:**
  - **Premature Conclusion:** The agent reaches a conclusion without sufficient evidence or only based on partial information.
  - **Misinterpretation of Information:** The agent misinterprets or misapplies information gathered from sources, leading to an incorrect conclusion.
  - **Overlooking Key Details:** The agent fails to consider important details or constraints provided in the question, such as the specific options available or the context of the patient's age.
  - **Inconsistent Reasoning:** The agent's reasoning process is inconsistent, changing from one step to another without clear justification.

- **Telltale Signs in Agent's Behavior or Responses:**
  - **Rapid Decision Making:** The agent makes a decision quickly without thorough analysis or multiple sources of information.
  - **Limited Information Gathering:** The agent relies on a single source or limited data to form a conclusion.
  - **Ignoring Task Requirements:** The agent does not follow the task instructions to gather and compile multiple sources of information.
  - **Logical Disconnect:** There is a logical disconnect between the gathered information and the final conclusion, often due to a leap in reasoning that is not supported by the data.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis
- **Contextual Conditions:**
  - **Complex Medical Scenarios:** The task involves a medical scenario with multiple potential causes or conditions.
  - **Multiple Choices:** The task provides multiple answer choices, some of which may be closely related or overlapping in symptoms.
  - **Reputable Sources Required:** The task explicitly requires the use of reputable medical sources to support the conclusion.
  - **Step-by-Step Analysis:** The task requires a step-by-step approach to gather and analyze information before making a final decision.

- **Sequence of Interactions:**
  - **Initial Hypothesis Formation:** The agent forms an initial hypothesis based on common medical knowledge or a quick search.
  - **Partial Information Gathering:** The agent gathers information from one or two sources but does not complete the required research.
  - **Conclusion Without Full Evidence:** The agent concludes with an answer without compiling or considering all the necessary evidence.
  - **Lack of Critical Evaluation:** The agent does not critically evaluate the information or consider the limitations of the sources.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics
- **Specific Questions:**
  - **"What evidence do you have to support your conclusion?"**
  - **"Have you considered all the available options in the question?"**
  - **"Can you explain the reasoning behind each step of your analysis?"**
  - **"Did you gather information from multiple reputable sources as instructed?"**
  - **"How does the information from your sources directly relate to the options provided in the question?"**

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Evaluate the Completeness of Information:** Check if the agent has gathered information from all the specified sources and if the information is sufficient to make a well-informed decision.
  - **Cross-Reference Data:** Ensure that the data from different sources is cross-referenced and that the conclusions are consistent across these sources.
  - **Consider the Context of the Question:** Analyze whether the agent has taken into account all the contextual details provided in the question, such as the patient's symptoms, age, and the specific answer choices.
  - **Trace the Reasoning Process:** Review the agent's reasoning process step-by-step to identify any logical leaps or inconsistencies.
  - **Assess the Relevance of Sources:** Determine if the sources used by the agent are directly relevant to the question and if they provide strong evidence for the chosen answer.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - **"Based on common clinical scenarios..."** or **"In most cases..."**: These phrases may indicate that the agent is forming a conclusion based on general knowledge rather than specific evidence.
  - **"Therefore, the best answer appears to be..."** without supporting data: This pattern suggests that the agent is making a conclusion without sufficient evidence.
  - **"No prior specialist output yet."**: This phrase can indicate that the agent is proceeding without a complete understanding of the task requirements.
  - **"The strongest justification is that..."** without citing multiple sources: This may suggest that the agent is relying on a single piece of information to form a conclusion.
  - **"The task asks which examination will most likely uncover the source..."** followed by a conclusion that does not align with the gathered evidence: This indicates a misinterpretation of the information or a logical disconnect.

By using this template, future conversations can be analyzed to quickly identify similar patterns of error, ensuring more accurate and thorough responses.

### Error Schema for Identifying Similar Errors in Future Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Inconsistent State Descriptions:** The agent provides a correct state description for one element but an incorrect state description for another, despite having the necessary information to determine both correctly.
- **Premature Task Completion:** The agent declares the task complete and provides a final answer without fully verifying all the given options or statements.

- **Task Type:** The task involves comparing physical properties (like boiling or melting points) of elements to a given temperature or state.
- **Initial Interaction:** The task is presented with a set of facts and a multiple-choice question.
- **Agent's Role:** The agent (Planner) is expected to analyze the given facts and options to determine the correct answer.
- **Sequence of Interactions:** The agent does not engage any specialists for further information, relying solely on the provided facts and their own reasoning to complete the task.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Verification of All Options:** Ask the agent to explicitly state their reasoning for each option, not just the ones they believe to be true.
  - *Example Question:* "Can you please provide the reasoning for why each of the options A, B, C, and D are true or false?"
- **Cross-Check Information:** Ensure the agent has considered all relevant physical properties (boiling point, melting point, etc.) for each element mentioned.
  - *Example Question:* "Have you verified the boiling and melting points for both oxygen and bromine in relation to room temperature?"
- **Critical Information Review:** Prompt the agent to review and confirm the critical information provided in the task.
  - *Example Question:* "Can you confirm the room temperature and the states of oxygen and bromine at this temperature?"
- **Logical Consistency:** Check if the agent's conclusions are logically consistent with the provided facts.
  - *Example Question:* "Are your conclusions about the states of oxygen and bromine at room temperature consistent with the boiling and melting points you have stated?"
- **Key Phrases for Indicators:**
  - "The task is complete."
  - "Final answer: [Partially correct answer]."
  - "No prior specialist interaction has occurred."
  - "Remember to break down the task and gather information systematically."
  - "Build upon previous results."
  - *Conversation Pattern:* The agent quickly jumps to a conclusion without a thorough analysis of all provided options.

### Analytical Framework:
- **Step-by-Step Analysis:** Each physical property (boiling point, melting point) should be compared to the room temperature for all elements mentioned.
- **Consistency Check:** Ensure that the agent's reasoning for each option is consistent with the known physical properties of the elements.
- **Complete Verification:** The agent should verify the correctness of each statement in the multiple-choice question, not just the ones they initially think are correct.

### Example Application:
- **Identifying the Error:** If the agent states that the task is complete and provides a final answer without addressing all the options, this is a potential error signature.
- **Context Analysis:** The task involves a comparison of physical properties, and the agent has not engaged any specialists for additional verification.
- **Heuristics Application:** Ask the agent to provide a detailed reasoning for each option and cross-check the critical information about room temperature and the states of the elements. Look for logical consistency in their conclusions.

  - **Direct Response Without Breakdown:** The planner directly responds to the user's question without breaking down the task into smaller, manageable steps.
  - **No Specialist Engagement:** The planner does not engage any of the available specialists (WebSurfer, FileSurfer, Coder, CodeExecutor) to gather necessary information or perform required analyses.
  - **Task Completion Before Information Gathering:** The planner marks the task as complete before any information has been gathered or analyzed, indicating a lack of thoroughness.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics
- **Specific Questions:**
  - **"Did you consult any of the specialists before providing your answer?"**
  - **"Can you explain how you arrived at the conclusion of commensalism?"**
  - **"What evidence do you have to support your classification of the relationship?"**
  - **"Have you considered the other options and their definitions in your analysis?"**

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Evaluate the Use of Specialists:** Check if the agent has used any of the available specialists to gather or verify information.
  - **Assess Depth of Analysis:** Determine if the agent's response includes a detailed breakdown of the task and a systematic approach to analyzing the options.
  - **Verify Ecological Definitions:** Ensure that the agent correctly understands and applies the ecological terms (parasitism, saprophytism, mutualism, commensalism).
  - **Check for Premature Conclusion:** Identify if the agent has marked the task as complete without sufficient evidence or analysis.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - **"This pattern corresponds to..."** without supporting evidence or detailed explanation.
  - **"No specialist has yet produced an output."** followed by a decision that does not involve specialist input.
  - **"TASK_COMPLETE"** issued before any data gathering or verification steps.
  - **"Final Answer:"** provided without prior consultation or analysis steps.
  - **Assumption of Minimal Impact:** Phrases like **"the host may experience negligible fitness impact"** without empirical support or consideration of the broader context.

### Application of the Template
To apply this template in future conversations, review the agent's responses for the presence of the error signatures and contextual conditions. If these are observed, use the detection heuristics to ask specific questions and apply an analytical framework to confirm the presence of the error. Key phrases and conversation patterns should also be monitored as they can serve as early indicators of potential missteps in the reasoning process.

    - "If any page blocks content, use the best accessible quotes and URLs."

- **Inconsistent Physical Reasoning:**
  - The agent's response contradicts the basic principles of physics, particularly the relationship between velocity and acceleration.
  - The agent may state that a physical quantity changes direction or sign without a logical or physical basis for the change.
- **Overlooked Key Information:**
  - The agent does not properly account for all given information in the problem, leading to an incorrect conclusion.
  - The agent might ignore the effect of external forces (like drag) on the motion of the object.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Contextual Conditions:**
  - The conversation involves a scenario where an object's motion changes due to an external force (e.g., a parachute opening).
  - The task requires an understanding of basic physics concepts, including the signs of velocity and acceleration.
- **Preceding Interactions:**
  - The user or another agent has provided clear definitions or conditions for the signs of the physical quantities (e.g., "velocity and acceleration are both considered positive before the chute opens").
  - The agent makes a decision or provides an answer without engaging in systematic information gathering or analysis (e.g., not using available capabilities like WebSurfer or Coder to verify or compute the physical quantities).

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions:**

  - "How does the direction of acceleration change when the chute opens, and why?"

  - "What external forces are acting on the object, and how do they affect the motion?"
- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Step-by-Step Analysis:**
    - Identify the initial conditions (signs of velocity and acceleration).
    - Determine the effect of the external force (e.g., parachute opening) on the object's motion.
    - Analyze the change in velocity and acceleration based on the direction of the external force and the initial motion.
  - **Verification of Physical Principles:**
    - Ensure that the agent's reasoning aligns with the laws of physics, particularly Newton's laws of motion.
    - Check if the agent has considered the role of forces and the resulting changes in motion (deceleration, direction change).
- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - Phrases indicating a misunderstanding of direction and sign:
    - "Velocity becomes negative while acceleration remains positive."
    - "Acceleration is still positive because..."
    - "The object slows down, so..."
  - Patterns of overlooked information:
    - The agent does not mention or consider the effect of drag or air resistance.
    - The agent skips steps in the reasoning process, jumping directly to a conclusion without intermediate logical steps.
  - Phrases showing inconsistent reasoning:
    - "Since the object is moving downward, it should be..."
    - "The chute opens, and therefore..."
    - "Given that the velocity is positive, the acceleration must be..."

  - The user presents a multiple-choice question with clear options.
  - The Planner agent analyzes the question and immediately selects an answer without engaging in systematic information gathering.
  - The Planner agent provides a detailed explanation for the chosen answer, which, while correct, may not align well with the options provided.
  - The Planner agent declares the task complete without considering the need for further verification or exploration of other options.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions to Determine if the Error is Present:**
  - "Did the agent systematically evaluate all the given options before selecting the final answer?"
  - "Is the selected answer the most direct and relevant effect, or is it a more complex or less common one?"
  - "Does the agent's explanation focus narrowly on a specific aspect of the interaction, potentially missing other relevant points?"

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Option Evaluation:** Check if the agent has considered and evaluated each multiple-choice option in the context of the question.
  - **Relevance and Directness:** Assess whether the chosen answer is the most relevant and direct effect, as opposed to a secondary or more complex effect.
  - **Premature Conclusion:** Determine if the agent concluded the task too quickly without a thorough analysis or verification step.
  - **Overthinking:** Identify if the agent's reasoning is overly detailed, suggesting a focus on deeper, less immediately relevant information.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - Phrases indicating a quick conclusion: "The answer is clearly..." or "Based on this, the answer must be..."
  - Phrases suggesting overthinking: "Among the options, this is the more direct thermodynamic effect..." or "This is a complex process involving..."
  - Lack of systematic evaluation: "No previous specialist output" followed by an immediate decision without further steps.
  - Bias towards complexity: "The other options are secondary or indirect compared to..."

By using this template, you can systematically identify if the Planner agent is making similar errors in future conversations, particularly in tasks involving multiple-choice questions or requiring a balanced approach to information gathering and decision-making.

### Error Schema for Identifying Similar Errors in Future Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Distinctive Patterns or Signals:**
  - The agent provides a final answer that is not directly listed among the given options.
  - The agent introduces new terms or phrases that are not mentioned in the original question (e.g., "fruit production rate" instead of "number of fruits").
  - The agent declares the task complete without fully addressing the provided options.
  - The agent's response shows a misunderstanding of basic experimental design terminology (e.g., dependent vs. independent variables).

- **Telltale Signs in the Agent's Behavior or Responses:**
  - The agent does not acknowledge or explicitly consider the provided options in the response.
  - The agent's answer is more detailed or complex than necessary, suggesting overthinking or misinterpretation.
  - The agent fails to recognize the straightforward nature of the question and instead provides an elaborate or incorrect explanation.
  - The agent may skip the step of identifying the context of the question, leading to an answer that does not match the question's structure.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Contextual Conditions:**
  - The question involves a simple identification of variables in an experiment.
  - The options are clearly defined and limited to specific terms.
  - The experiment setup is described with a clear independent variable and dependent variable.
  - The agent is expected to choose from the provided options without needing additional information or capabilities.

- **Sequence of Interactions:**
  - The user presents a straightforward question with a set of options.
  - The agent does not engage in any information gathering or analysis steps.
  - The agent directly provides a final answer.
  - The agent's answer is not one of the provided options and introduces new, unrelated terms.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions:**
  - "Does the final answer match any of the provided options exactly?"
  - "Did the agent introduce new terms or concepts not present in the original question?"
  - "Is the agent's response more complex than the question requires?"
  - "Did the agent acknowledge all the given options before providing a final answer?"

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Option Verification:** Check if the agent's final answer is one of the options provided in the question.
  - **Term Consistency:** Compare the terms used in the agent's response to those in the question to identify any discrepancies.
  - **Complexity Assessment:** Evaluate whether the agent's response introduces unnecessary complexity or overthinking.
  - **Context Recognition:** Determine if the agent has correctly identified and understood the context of the question, particularly in terms of experimental design.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - **Phrases Indicating Misunderstanding:**
    - "The dependent variable is [new term or concept]."
    - "The task is complete, and the answer is [not a provided option]."
  - **Patterns in Response Structure:**
    - The agent's response lacks a clear reference to the provided options.
    - The agent's explanation is lengthy and deviates from the simple structure of the question.
    - The agent's answer is not aligned with the expected format (e.g., choosing a letter from A to D).

### Application of the Template:
- **When a user asks a question with specific options:**
  - Verify that the agent's response matches one of the provided options.
  - Look for any new terms introduced by the agent that are not in the question.
  - Assess if the agent's response is overly complex or detailed.
  - Ensure the agent has considered all given options before declaring the task complete.

- **If the agent introduces new terms or concepts:**
  - Cross-check these terms with the original question to see if they were explicitly asked for or if they are a misinterpretation.
  - Ask the agent to explain why the new term is necessary and how it relates to the provided options.

- **If the agent's response is complex:**
  - Simplify the question and ask the agent to provide a direct, concise answer.
  - Request the agent to identify the dependent variable without adding extra information.

- **If the agent skips context recognition:**
  - Prompt the agent to restate the experiment setup and identify the variables clearly.
  - Ask the agent to explain the role of each variable in the context of the experiment.

By applying these heuristics, similar errors can be identified and addressed in future conversations, ensuring that the agent's responses are aligned with the user's expectations and the provided options.

### Error Schema for Identifying Similar Errors in Future Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Misinterpretation of the Question**: The agent provides an answer that is not listed among the options given in the question. This indicates that the agent has either misunderstood the question or created a new, non-relevant answer.
- **Inconsistent Logical Flow**: The agent's reasoning process does not logically follow from the context provided. For example, the agent discusses concepts like reliability and error reduction which are not directly related to the building designs' response to earthquakes.
- **Focus on Methodology Over Outcome**: The agent's response emphasizes the methodology of testing (e.g., replication, reducing random error) rather than the outcomes of the tests as specified in the question options.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Task Type**: The task involves selecting the most likely outcome from a list of options based on a specific scenario or experiment.
- **Preceding Interaction**: The agent is asked to break down the task, gather information systematically, and build upon previous results. However, the agent skips these steps and directly provides a final answer.
- **Lack of Relevance to Options**: The agent does not reference or consider the provided options (A, B, C, D) in their reasoning, leading to an answer that does not align with the expected choices.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Verification of Answer Options**: Ask, "Does the final answer provided by the agent match any of the given options in the question?"
- **Contextual Relevance Check**: Evaluate if the agent's reasoning and final answer are directly relevant to the context and specific outcomes mentioned in the question.
- **Methodology vs. Outcome Focus**: Determine if the agent's response focuses more on the methodology of the experiment rather than the potential outcomes as asked in the question.
- **Logical Consistency**: Check if the agent's explanation logically leads to the final answer. If the reasoning is abstract or unrelated to the concrete outcomes, it may indicate an error.
- **Key Phrases for Misinterpretation**: Look for phrases like "reliability," "replication," "random error," or "standard error" in the context of a question that asks about practical outcomes of an experiment or test.
- **Behavioral Patterns**: Note if the agent frequently:
  - Skips steps in the task breakdown.
  - Provides answers that are technically correct but not aligned with the question's intent.
  - Fails to engage with the provided options, instead creating a new answer.

### Example Questions for Error Detection:
- "Can you confirm that the final answer you provided is one of the options listed in the question?"
- "How does your final answer relate to the potential outcomes mentioned in the question?"
- "Could you explain how the methodology of testing multiple building designs directly leads to the outcome you selected?"

### Analytical Framework:
1. **Option Matching**:
   - Identify if the agent's final answer is a direct match to one of the provided options.
   - If not, flag for potential misinterpretation or misunderstanding.

2. **Contextual Relevance**:
   - Assess whether the agent's reasoning and answer are contextually relevant to the question.
   - If the answer discusses aspects not mentioned in the options (e.g., reliability, error reduction), it may be off-target.

3. **Outcome Focus**:
   - Ensure the agent's response is focused on the outcomes of the experiment (what will likely result) rather than the methodology (how the test is conducted).
   - If the methodology is overemphasized, it suggests the agent might be missing the point of the question.

4. **Logical Flow**:
   - Examine the logical progression from the task breakdown to the final answer.
   - If the steps are missing or the reasoning is not clear, it may indicate an error in understanding or execution.

### Key Phrases and Patterns:
- **Phrases Indicating Methodology Over Outcome**:
  - "reliability"
  - "replication"
  - "random error"
  - "standard error"
- **Patterns in Responses**:
  - The agent provides a final answer without referencing the given options.
  - The agent skips detailed task breakdown and information gathering, jumping straight to a conclusion.
  - The agent's explanation is more abstract and less practical, focusing on statistical or methodological aspects rather than the specific outcomes of the experiment.

By using this template, you can systematically identify when an agent is likely to make similar errors in future conversations, particularly in tasks involving the selection of outcomes from a list of options based on a given scenario.

- **Telltale Signs in the Agent's Behavior or Responses:**

  - The agent's reasoning lacks a balanced consideration of all possible factors and tends to focus on a single, often overly generalized, aspect.
  - The agent may disregard or minimize the importance of peer review, shared standards, and the iterative nature of scientific research in their rationale.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Contextual Conditions:**
  - The question involves a topic with inherent complexity or multiple contributing factors (e.g., scientific disagreement, which can be due to various reasons including methodology, interpretation, and experimental design).
  - The options provided are nuanced, with some being partially correct but not the best answer, and others being overly broad or incorrect.
  - The conversation is at the initial stage where the task is being analyzed and the first action is being decided.

- **Sequence of Interactions:**
  - The agent is asked to analyze the task and decide on the first action without having any prior specialist input or context.
  - The agent provides a rationale that is based on a cursory understanding of the topic, leading to a premature or overly simplistic conclusion.
  - The agent claims the task is complete and provides a final answer, bypassing the need for further investigation or consultation with other specialists.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions to Determine if the Error is Present:**
  - "Can you explain why you chose this option over the others?"
  - "Are there any specific examples or evidence that support your rationale?"
  - "How does your choice account for the complexities and nuances in the question?"
  - "Have you considered the role of peer review and shared standards in scientific investigations?"

- **Analytical Framework:**

  - **Context Sensitivity:** Determine if the agent's response is sensitive to the specific context and details provided in the question, or if it oversimplifies the issue.
  - **Premature Conclusion:** Check if the agent has claimed the task is complete without engaging in a systematic process of information gathering and analysis.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - **Broad Generalizations:** Phrases like "generally flawed," "often have limitations," or "always lead to" that do not account for the specific context or nuances.
  - **Overconfidence:** Statements like "TASK_COMPLETE" or "Final Answer" that indicate the agent believes they have sufficient information to conclude without further steps.
  - **Lack of Specificity:** Rationales that do not cite specific examples or mechanisms (e.g., "methodological limitations" without specifying what these limitations are or how they affect conclusions).
  - **Ignoring Nuanced Options:** Dismissing options that are more specific or nuanced with reasons that are not well-founded or overly simplistic (e.g., "C is less central because...").

### Template Application:

- **Rationale Evaluation:** If the agent's reasoning is shallow or fails to consider the full context and complexity of the issue.
- **Premature Task Completion:** If the agent claims the task is complete without engaging in systematic information gathering or consulting relevant specialists.
- **Key Phrases:** If the agent uses phrases that indicate overgeneralization, overconfidence, or a lack of specificity in their response.

### Error Schema for Identifying Experimental Design Errors in Multi-Agent Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Misidentification of Variables:** The agent incorrectly identifies the variable that should be kept constant as the one being tested.
- **Premature Conclusion:** The agent concludes the task is complete without considering all the options or the full context of the experiment.
- **Logical Inconsistency:** The agent's reasoning does not align with the principles of experimental design, particularly in terms of isolating the variable of interest.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Task Type:** The task involves a multiple-choice question about experimental design, typically in a science or physics context.
- **Initial Presentation:** The user presents the task with a clear statement of the hypothesis and the experimental procedure.
- **Agent's Initial Response:** The agent responds quickly, often without requesting additional information or clarification.
- **Lack of Step-by-Step Analysis:** The agent does not break down the task into smaller steps or consider the role of each variable in the experiment.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Questions to Determine Error Presence:**
  - "What is the independent variable in this experiment?"
  - "What is the dependent variable in this experiment?"
  - "Can you explain why you chose this variable to keep constant?"
  - "What other variables are present in the experiment, and how might they affect the results?"

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Identify the Hypothesis:** Determine what Ben is trying to test.
  - **List All Variables:** Identify all variables mentioned in the experiment (e.g., amount of air, drop distance, drop time, bounce count).
  - **Determine Variable Roles:** Classify each variable as independent, dependent, or a controlled variable.
  - **Check for Consistency:** Ensure the agent's choice aligns with keeping the controlled variables constant and the independent variable varied.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - "The variable being tested is..."
  - "To ensure the replicate trials are comparable..."
  - "The task is complete..."
  - "The effect of [incorrect variable] can be isolated..."
  - "No prior specialist output..."

This template can be used to quickly identify when an agent is making errors in understanding and applying the principles of experimental design, particularly in the context of a multiple-choice question where the correct variable to control is crucial for the validity of the experiment.

=== Schema for Error Log 94 ===
Generated Schema:
### Error Schema for Error Identification in Multi-Agent Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Repetition of Information**: The agent repeatedly cites the same or similar information from a single source without expanding or deepening the understanding of the task.
- **Misalignment with Task Requirements**: The agent's responses or actions do not align with the specific requirements of the task, such as focusing on a different aspect of the question (e.g., well-being in remote work instead of information access).
- **Inadequate Source Diversity**: The agent fails to gather a diverse set of credible sources, instead focusing on a narrow range of sources or a single source.
- **Lack of Explicit Evidence**: The agent provides sources that are inferential or tangential rather than explicitly supporting the required aspect of the task (e.g., general statements about the Internet without specific evidence on information access from anywhere).

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Task Complexity**: The task involves a multiple-choice question where the correct answer is not immediately obvious and requires gathering and synthesizing evidence from various sources.
- **Initial Source Selection**: The initial search or source selection is based on a broad or vague query, leading to less relevant results.
- **Sequential Missteps**: The agent continues to pursue a specific angle (e.g., remote work) even after receiving feedback that suggests a different angle (e.g., information access) is more relevant.
- **Over-Reliance on Single Source**: The agent relies heavily on a single source or type of source (e.g., only using Britannica or Pew Research Center) without considering other credible options.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Ask for Source Diversity**:
  - "Have you considered sources from other reputable organizations, such as UNESCO, ITU, or the World Bank?"
  - "Can you provide at least two distinct sources that support the chosen option?"
- **Check Alignment with Task Requirements**:
  - "Does the information you gathered directly address the question about how the Internet has improved life?"
  - "Can you summarize how each source explicitly supports the idea of the Internet enabling access to information from many locations?"
- **Evaluate Evidence Strength**:
  - "Are the sources you found providing direct evidence or are they inferential?"
  - "Can you find any sources that explicitly state the benefits of the Internet in terms of information access from multiple locations?"
- **Identify Key Phrases**:
  - "The Internet enables people to share information and communicate from anywhere with an Internet connection."
  - "Access to information, including through the Internet, is widely recognised as an enabler of a broad range of human rights."
  - "An integrated, open and inclusive digital library can make e-resources available to anyone, anytime, anywhere."
  - "The urgent task ahead is ubiquitous connectivity—ideally, an internet connection that is always available."
- **Detect Narrow Focus**:
  - "Why did you choose to focus on remote work instead of information access?"
  - "Can you provide a broader range of benefits that the Internet has brought to people's lives, particularly in terms of accessing information from multiple locations?"
- **Analyze Source Relevance**:
  - "How does the information from this source relate to the specific benefits of the Internet mentioned in the question?"
  - "Are there any sources that discuss the broader impact of the Internet on information access, rather than just its impact on work or specific sectors?"

### Example of Error Pattern:
- **Initial Query**: The agent starts with a broad or vague query that does not directly address the task requirements.
- **Source Selection**: The agent selects a source that provides general information about the Internet but does not explicitly support the chosen option (e.g., Britannica's general description of the Internet).
- **Sequential Actions**: Despite receiving feedback suggesting a different angle, the agent continues to gather sources that support the initial, less relevant angle (e.g., focusing on remote work benefits).
- **Synthesis**: The agent synthesizes information that is inferential rather than explicit, leading to a weak justification for the chosen option.

### Key Indicators:
- **Repetitive Content**: Multiple searches yield similar or identical information from the same source.
- **Narrow Scope**: The agent's responses focus on a single aspect of the task, ignoring broader or more relevant angles.
- **Weak Justification**: The agent's synthesis lacks strong, explicit evidence and relies on inferential statements.
- **Source Overlap**: The agent uses the same or very similar sources repeatedly, without exploring other reputable options.

### Analytical Framework:
- **Source Relevance Check**: Evaluate whether the sources directly address the task requirements.
- **Diversity of Sources**: Ensure that the agent is using a diverse set of credible sources.
- **Evidence Strength Analysis**: Determine if the evidence is explicit and strong, or if it is inferential and weak.
- **Task Alignment**: Verify that the agent's actions and responses align with the specific requirements of the task.

=== Schema for Error Log 95 ===
Generated Schema:
### Error Schema for Identifying Similar Errors in Future Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Misinterpretation of Thermodynamic Signs:** The agent incorrectly interprets the thermodynamic signs (e.g., q < 0) as indicative of an exothermic reaction when the question clearly states that the surroundings get colder, which should suggest an endothermic process.
- **Confusion Between Heat Flow Directions:** The agent confuses the direction of heat flow, assuming that heat loss from the surroundings means the reaction is exothermic, instead of recognizing that the reaction is absorbing heat (endothermic).
- **Inappropriate Application of Thermochemistry Principles:** The agent applies principles of exothermic reactions to a scenario that describes endothermic behavior, showing a lack of understanding of the fundamental difference between the two.
- **Ignoring Non-Thermodynamic Options:** The agent dismisses options that are not related to heat flow (e.g., suspension, vaporization) without considering the possibility that the question might be testing the understanding of these terms in a different context.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Type of Task:** The task is a multiple-choice question that involves basic knowledge of chemistry, specifically the classification of chemical reactions based on heat flow.
- **Lack of Initial Information:** The error occurs in the first step of the conversation, indicating that the agent did not properly analyze the initial information provided.
- **Decision-Making Without Verification:** The agent makes a decision and marks the task as complete without verifying the correctness of the answer through additional resources or capabilities.
- **Context of Thermochemistry:** The conversation revolves around the concepts of exothermic and endothermic reactions, which are fundamental to understanding chemical processes and heat flow.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Questions to Ask:**
  - "Does the agent's response correctly interpret the direction of heat flow as described in the question?"
  - "Is the agent's understanding of exothermic and endothermic reactions consistent with the context of the question?"
  - "Has the agent considered all the options provided, or are some options dismissed without proper reasoning?"
  - "Does the agent's explanation align with the basic principles of thermochemistry?"

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Step-by-Step Verification:** Check if the agent has broken down the task into steps and verified each step logically.
  - **Heat Flow Direction Analysis:** Specifically analyze the agent's interpretation of heat flow direction (q < 0 or q > 0) and its relation to the reaction type.
  - **Option Elimination Reasoning:** Evaluate the reasoning behind the elimination of each option to ensure it is based on accurate knowledge and not assumptions.
  - **Conceptual Consistency:** Ensure that the agent's explanation is conceptually consistent with the principles of exothermic and endothermic reactions.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - "When a chemical reaction produces a drop in temperature (surroundings get colder), the surroundings have lost heat, so q_surroundings < 0."
  - "Exothermic processes are characterized by negative heat (q < 0)."
  - "The observed cooling indicates heat loss (q < 0), consistent with exothermic behavior."
  - "Final Answer: A) exothermic."
  - "No previous result exists. This is a straightforward knowledge question."
  - "TASK_COMPLETE" without further verification or use of available capabilities.

### Template Summary
This template is designed to help identify errors where an agent misinterprets the direction of heat flow in a chemical reaction, leading to an incorrect classification of the reaction as exothermic when it should be endothermic. The template focuses on recognizing patterns in the agent's initial analysis, decision-making, and response to ensure that such misunderstandings can be detected early in future conversations.

### Error Schema for Error Identification in Multi-Agent Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Overgeneralization of Facts:** The agent incorrectly generalizes a specific or limited fact to a broader, more universal claim.
- **Misinterpretation of Terms:** The agent misinterprets or applies a term in a way that is not consistent with its common or technical definition.
- **Lack of Nuance:** The agent fails to recognize the nuanced nature of the information, leading to an oversimplified or incorrect conclusion.
- **Premature Conclusion:** The agent reaches a conclusion based on partial or incomplete information, without fully verifying all aspects of the task.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Task Type:** The task involves verifying the factual accuracy of multiple statements, particularly those that can be subjective or require specific context.
- **Agent Role:** The Planner is responsible for analyzing and synthesizing information from various sources to make a decision.
- **Interaction Sequence:**
  - The WebSurfer provides information from a credible source that partially supports one of the options.
  - The Planner interprets this partial support as a definitive confirmation without considering the full context or other sources.
  - The Planner fails to critically evaluate the generalizability of the information provided.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions to Ask:**
  - "Does the information provided fully support the general claim being made, or is it specific to a subset of the subject?"
  - "Are there any conditions or exceptions mentioned in the sources that should be considered?"
  - "Is the term being used in a consistent manner across all sources, or is there a potential for misinterpretation?"
  - "Have all possible aspects of the task been verified before reaching a conclusion?"

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Evaluate the Scope of Information:** Determine whether the information is broad or specific. Broad claims should be supported by broad evidence, while specific claims need specific evidence.
  - **Cross-Check Multiple Sources:** Verify that the information is consistent across different credible sources. Discrepancies or additional context can help identify overgeneralizations.
  - **Consider Exceptions and Variations:** Look for any exceptions or variations in the information that might affect the general claim. For example, if a fact applies to only a few species, it should not be generalized to all species.
  - **Assess the Interpretation of Key Terms:** Ensure that key terms (e.g., "freezing temperatures," "fierce competitors") are interpreted correctly and consistently with their common or technical definitions.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - **Generalizing from Specific Examples:** Phrases like "This supports the notion that" or "This confirms that" when followed by a broad, generalized statement.
  - **Ignoring Nuances:** Phrases like "However, this is not strictly factual" or "This undercuts the general claim" indicating that the agent has overlooked important details.
  - **Premature Conclusion:** Phrases like "Based on this, we can conclude" or "Final Answer:" appearing too early in the conversation, before all information has been gathered and evaluated.
  - **Subjective Interpretation:** Phrases like "supported by ethological observations" or "subjective opinion" when used to justify a factual claim, indicating a potential confusion between subjective and objective information.

### Example Application of the Template:
- **Scenario:** A similar task is given to identify which statement is a fact about a different animal (e.g., polar bears).
- **Detection:**
  - **Error Signature:** The Planner might overgeneralize a fact about polar bears living in cold regions to all polar bears living in "freezing temperatures" without considering the specific climates of their habitats.
  - **Context Analysis:** The task involves multiple statements, and the WebSurfer provides information from a credible source that partially supports one statement. The Planner then generalizes this partial support.
  - **Heuristics:**
    - **Specific Questions:** "Does the source specify the exact temperature ranges where polar bears live?" "Are there any polar bear species that live in climates that are not consistently freezing?"
    - **Analytical Framework:** Check if the information is broadly applicable or specific to certain conditions. Cross-verify with other sources to ensure consistency.
    - **Key Phrases:** "This supports the notion that polar bears can live in freezing temperatures" or "Based on this, we can conclude that polar bears are found in freezing climates."

By using this template, future conversations can be analyzed to detect similar patterns of overgeneralization, misinterpretation, and premature conclusions, ensuring a more accurate and nuanced approach to fact verification.
