### Error Schema for Error Detection: Missing Library or Module

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Distinctive Patterns or Signals:**
  - **Traceback Error:** The conversation includes a traceback error message indicating a `ModuleNotFoundError` or similar.
  - **Failed Import Attempt:** The agent reports an error during the import of a specific library or module (e.g., `matplotlib`, `pandas`, `numpy`).
  - **Functionality Failure:** The agent mentions that a certain functionality (e.g., plotting, data manipulation) cannot be executed due to a missing library or module.
  - **Conditional Execution:** The agent's code includes conditional logic to handle the absence of a library or module, indicating a potential fallback mechanism.

- **Telltale Signs in Agent's Behavior or Responses:**
  - **Request for Fallback:** The agent suggests a fallback approach (e.g., saving data to a CSV file) if the primary method (e.g., plotting) fails.
  - **Incomplete Output:** The agent provides only a subset of the expected outputs (e.g., numerical results but no plot).
  - **Error Handling Code:** The agent includes error handling code in the script to manage the absence of the library or module.
  - **Library Installation Suggestion:** The agent offers a quick command to install the missing library or module (e.g., `pip install matplotlib`).

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Contextual Conditions:**
  - **Environment Constraints:** The error occurs in an execution environment that lacks the necessary libraries or modules, often due to a minimal or constrained setup.
  - **Code Execution:** The error is triggered when the agent attempts to execute code that relies on external libraries or modules.
  - **Initial Plan Involves Plotting:** The initial plan or instructions involve generating visual outputs, which require plotting libraries like `matplotlib`.

- **Sequence of Interactions:**
  - **Plan Specification:** The Planner specifies a plan that includes generating plots or using specific libraries.
  - **Code Implementation:** The Coder implements the plan, including the necessary imports.
  - **Code Execution:** The CodeExecutor runs the script and encounters an error due to the missing library or module.
  - **Error Reporting:** The CodeExecutor reports the error back to the Planner, who then adjusts the plan to account for the missing library or module.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions:**
  - "Did the script fail to import any libraries or modules?"
  - "Is there a `ModuleNotFoundError` or similar error in the traceback?"
  - "What outputs were you able to produce, and what outputs are missing?"
  - "Are there any conditional statements in the code to handle missing libraries?"

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Code Review:** Check the code for import statements and identify which libraries or modules are being used.
  - **Environment Check:** Verify the execution environment's setup to ensure all required dependencies are installed.
  - **Output Verification:** Compare the actual outputs with the expected outputs to identify missing elements (e.g., plots, specific data files).

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - **Error Phrases:**
    - "ModuleNotFoundError: No module named '..."
    - "Failed to import..."
    - "Plotting is not available..."
    - "Could not proceed with plotting..."
  - **Fallback Indicators:**
    - "If plotting is unavailable, it will save the data to a CSV file..."
    - "Falling back to CSV output..."
    - "Saving data to CSV for external plotting..."
  - **Library Installation Suggestions:**
    - "You can install the missing library using `pip install ...`"
    - "If you have conda, try `conda install ...`"
    - "Ensure the required libraries are installed..."

This template can be used to quickly identify when a conversation is encountering issues due to missing libraries or modules, allowing for more efficient problem recognition and subsequent planning adjustments.

  - **Relevance of Analysis:** Evaluate whether the agent's reasoning and provided information directly address the primary environmental concern mentioned in the question.
  - **Use of Specialists:** Verify if the agent utilized the available specialists to gather information, especially when the task suggests the need for external data or analysis.
  - **Premature Task Completion:** Assess if the agent declared the task complete too quickly, without exploring all the necessary aspects of the question.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - "No prior results exist" followed by a detailed conclusion.
  - "TASK_COMPLETE" or "Final Answer" in the first response.
  - "Because [specific external fact], the most likely benefit is [option]" without linking it back to the question's context.
  - Use of terms like "scoring model," "quantitative analysis," or "factors" in a response where the task does not explicitly ask for such methods.
  - Lack of mention of or interaction with other specialists (WebSurfer, FileSurfer, etc.).

This template can be used to quickly identify if an agent is making a similar premature and overly specific error in future conversations by focusing on the above signatures, contextual conditions, and heuristics.

### Error Schema for Identifying Similar Errors in Future Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Premature Conclusion:** The planner quickly concludes the correct answer without sufficient evidence or input from other agents.
- **Lack of Diverse Sources:** The planner relies on a single or limited number of sources to justify the answer, particularly when the task involves verifying a scientific or factual claim.
- **Misaligned Queries:** The planner’s search queries are not directly aligned with the core question, leading to irrelevant or tangential results.
- **Overlooking Relevance:** The planner overlooks the relevance of certain options (e.g., keeping a record of all procedures) in favor of a preferred answer, even when the evidence is not yet clear.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Task Type:** The task involves a multiple-choice question where the correct answer requires verification from credible sources.
- **Initial Assumption:** The planner makes an initial assumption about the correct answer without a thorough analysis of the options provided.
- **Sequential Searches:** The planner initiates multiple searches but does not adjust the queries based on the results, leading to repeated failures in finding relevant information.
- **Credibility Filters:** The planner applies strict filters (e.g., .edu sites only) early in the search process, which may limit the availability of relevant sources.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Question for Premature Conclusion:**
  - "Have we gathered sufficient evidence from multiple credible sources to justify the answer?"
  - "Are there any other options that might also be correct and need further investigation?"

- **Question for Lack of Diverse Sources:**
  - "How many different credible sources have we consulted to support the chosen answer?"
  - "Do the sources we have found cover a wide range of reputable institutions or are they all from the same type of source?"

- **Question for Misaligned Queries:**
  - "Are the search queries directly addressing the core question?"
  - "What specific keywords or phrases should be included in the search to ensure relevance?"

- **Question for Overlooking Relevance:**
  - "Why is the chosen option more important than the other options?"
  - "What evidence do we have that the other options are not also necessary for the task?"

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Step-by-Step Verification:** Ensure that each step in the decision-making process is justified with evidence from credible sources.
  - **Option Evaluation:** Systematically evaluate each multiple-choice option to determine its relevance and necessity.
  - **Query Adjustment:** Adjust search queries based on the results of previous searches to refine and focus the information gathering process.
  - **Source Diversity:** Aim to gather information from a diverse set of sources, including universities, reputable science-education resources, and peer-reviewed articles.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - **Premature Conclusion:** "We should proceed with the chosen answer now," "The correct answer is clearly X," without supporting evidence.
  - **Lack of Diverse Sources:** "We have one source from [specific institution] that supports this," "The [single source] is enough to justify the answer."
  - **Misaligned Queries:** "Search for [broad or tangential query]," "Look up [related but not directly relevant topic]."
  - **Overlooking Relevance:** "Option X is the best because it ensures [some aspect], but we haven’t looked at Y or Z."

By recognizing these patterns and asking these specific questions, future conversations can be analyzed to detect similar errors in the decision-making and information-gathering processes.

### Error Schema for Identifying Volume-Density Confusion Errors in Multi-Agent Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Distinctive Patterns or Signals:**
  - Incorrect application of density values, especially when comparing materials with vastly different densities.
  - Misinterpretation of the volume of materials leading to incorrect mass comparisons.
  - Use of incorrect or approximate density values for materials without verification.
  - Premature conclusion of the task without considering the need for accurate data or additional information.

- **Telltale Signs in the Agent's Behavior or Responses:**
  - The agent provides a final answer based on density values that are not verified or sourced.
  - The agent concludes that one material is heavier than the other without performing a calculation or checking the density of both materials.
  - The agent suggests using a formula (like m = rho * V) but applies it incorrectly or with incorrect data.
  - The agent skips steps in the logical reasoning process, such as not confirming the volume is the same or not converting units consistently.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Contextual Conditions:**
  - The task involves comparing the masses of two different materials based on their volume.
  - The materials have significantly different densities, and the correct answer hinges on this difference.
  - The task is presented in a multiple-choice format, which can sometimes lead to hasty decision-making.
  - The agent is expected to make a decision without direct access to the materials or a balance.

- **Sequence of Interactions:**
  - The agent is asked to analyze the task and decide on the first action.
  - Instead of breaking down the task or gathering necessary information, the agent directly applies a formula using assumed or incorrect density values.
  - The agent does not engage in a systematic approach to verify the density values or the assumptions made.
  - The agent quickly concludes the task without further analysis or consultation.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions:**
  - "What is the density of the materials you are comparing?"
  - "How did you determine the density values for the materials?"
  - "Can you verify the density values from a reliable source?"
  - "Did you consider the possibility that the materials might have different packing densities?"
  - "What units are you using for volume and density, and are they consistent?"

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Step-by-Step Reasoning Check:**
    - Identify if the agent has broken down the task into clear, logical steps.
    - Verify if the agent has correctly identified the need to compare densities.
    - Check if the agent has sourced density values from a reliable reference.
    - Ensure the agent has performed the necessary calculations to compare masses.
    - Confirm if the agent has considered the context of the materials (e.g., bulk density vs. individual density).

  - **Data Verification:**
    - Assess whether the agent has used accurate and up-to-date information.
    - Determine if the agent has cross-checked the density values with multiple sources or used a standard reference.
    - Evaluate if the agent has considered the physical properties of the materials in the context of the problem (e.g., how materials are packed).

  - **Premature Conclusion:**
    - Look for signs that the agent has reached a conclusion without a thorough analysis.
    - Check if the agent has skipped any steps that are necessary for a complete understanding of the problem.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - "Therefore, the mass of [material A] is greater than the mass of [material B]" without prior verification of density values.
  - "The masses cannot be compared without a balance" when the volumes are explicitly given and the task is to compare masses based on density.
  - "Using m = rho * V" followed by an incorrect or unverified density value.
  - "For 1 L, giving m_[material A] ~ [value] kg and m_[material B] ~ [value] kg" where the values are not logically consistent with known density properties.
  - "No previous result exists. Starting analysis from the problem statement" followed by an immediate conclusion without systematic data gathering or analysis.

This template can be used to recognize when an agent might be confusing or misapplying volume and density concepts, leading to incorrect mass comparisons.

- **Overemphasis on Microscopic Interactions:** The agent focuses on the microscopic (atomic or molecular) interactions (e.g., electromagnetic forces at the point of contact) rather than the macroscopic behavior of the system (e.g., mechanical wave transmission through rigid bodies).
- **Premature Conclusion:** The agent jumps to a conclusion without considering all possible wave types and their characteristics, leading to an incorrect final answer.
- **Lack of Physical Context:** The agent fails to recognize that the context of the problem is mechanical and not electromagnetic, despite the clear physical setup described.

- **Initial Task Setup:** The task involves a physical setup with multiple rigid bodies (e.g., bowling balls) in contact and a clear mechanical action (e.g., striking with a hammer).

- **Sequence of Interactions:** The error occurs in the first step of the task analysis, indicating a fundamental misunderstanding or misapplication of physical principles before any deeper investigation or reasoning.
- **Context of Rigid Body Mechanics:** The conversation lacks a proper discussion of the mechanics of rigid bodies and the nature of the forces involved in transmitting a wave through such a system.

- **Specific Questions:**
  - "What physical properties are you considering when classifying the wave type?"
  - "How does the macroscopic behavior of the system influence the type of wave transmitted?"
  - "Are there any mechanical wave types that could be relevant to this scenario?"
  - "Can you explain how the wave propagates through the bowling balls in terms of the overall system dynamics rather than just the atomic interactions?"

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Evaluate Macroscopic vs. Microscopic Focus:** Check if the agent's response emphasizes microscopic interactions (e.g., interatomic forces) over macroscopic behavior (e.g., movement of the balls).
  - **Assess Physical Mechanism Understanding:** Determine if the agent correctly understands the physical mechanism of wave transmission in the given scenario (e.g., mechanical vs. electromagnetic).
  - **Review Premature Conclusions:** Identify if the agent has made a conclusion based on limited or superficial analysis, without exploring all relevant options.
  - **Consider the Role of Medium:** Analyze whether the agent has considered the medium through which the wave is traveling and how it affects the wave type (e.g., solid, liquid, gas, or vacuum).

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - "The interaction is mediated by [microscopic force] at the interface."
  - "This rules out a purely mechanical [wave type] because [misleading microscopic reasoning]."
  - "The disturbance propagates as a [non-mechanical wave type] along the contact chain."
  - "The spheres behave as effectively rigid, discrete bodies without sustained [non-relevant physical property] variation in a continuous medium."

This template can be used to quickly identify when an agent is making a similar error in understanding the physical context of a problem and misclassifying wave types based on a focus on microscopic interactions rather than the macroscopic behavior of the system.

- **Sequence of Interactions:**
  - **Direct Response Without Delegation:** The agent responds directly without delegating the task to any of its capabilities.
  - **Lack of Information Gathering Steps:** The conversation lacks any steps where the agent would gather more information, such as performing a web search or reading a file.
  - **No Iterative Refinement:** The agent does not refine its understanding or answer based on feedback or additional data.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions to Determine the Error:**
  - "Did you consider all the options before deciding on the final answer?"
  - "Why did you choose not to use any of the available capabilities to gather more information?"
  - "Can you explain how each of the options (A, B, C, D) fits into your chosen category?"
  - "What specific properties of the options led you to conclude that they all belong to the same category?"

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Option Analysis:** Evaluate if the agent has considered the unique properties of each option in the multiple-choice question.
  - **Category Precision:** Check if the chosen category is precise enough to accurately describe all the options without overgeneralizing.
  - **Verification Steps:** Assess whether the agent has taken any steps to verify its answer, such as using WebSurfer or FileSurfer to find supporting information.
  - **Logical Consistency:** Examine the agent's reasoning for logical consistency and the accuracy of its generalizations about the options.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - "Therefore, the best unifying category is..."
  - "Based on my understanding, all of these are..."
  - "No previous specialist result exists for this task yet."
  - "I think the answer is..."
  - "TASK_COMPLETE" followed by a final answer without prior exploration steps.
  - "Please analyze this task and decide which specialist to engage first" followed by an immediate conclusion.

This template is designed to help identify errors in future conversations where an agent may prematurely conclude a task, overgeneralize categories, and fail to verify its answers through systematic information gathering.

=== Schema for Error Log 116 ===
Generated Schema:
### Error Schema for Error Identification in Multi-Agent Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures
- **Distinctive Patterns or Signals:**
  - **Repetitive Actions:** The agent repeatedly attempts to load the same source or perform the same search, indicating a potential issue with the source or the agent's ability to navigate.
  - **Timeout Messages:** The agent encounters timeout errors when attempting to load web pages, suggesting the source is either slow, unresponsive, or the agent's web surfing capabilities are limited.
  - **Partial Content Loading:** The agent provides partial content from the web page, often showing the same introductory text or metadata, which may indicate that the full content is not being loaded or accessed.

- **Telltale Signs in Agent's Behavior or Responses:**
  - **Redundant Information:** The agent reiterates information that has already been provided, without adding new insights or data.
  - **Inconsistent Conclusions:** The agent's conclusions vary or contradict each other, especially when the same source is cited multiple times.
  - **Error Logs:** The agent includes detailed error logs in the response, which are often related to web surfing or page loading issues.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis
- **Contextual Conditions:**
  - **Source Unavailability:** The error occurs when the agent is instructed to visit a specific web page that is either unresponsive or slow to load.
  - **High Load on Web Resources:** The error may occur during periods of high traffic or server load on the web resources being accessed.
  - **Technical Limitations:** The agent's technical capabilities may be insufficient to handle the web surfing task, leading to repeated failures.

- **Sequence of Interactions:**
  - **Initial Search:** The agent is initially instructed to perform a web search or visit a specific web page.
  - **Loading Attempts:** The agent attempts to load the page multiple times, each attempt resulting in a timeout or partial content loading.
  - **Reiteration of Instructions:** The Planner reiterates the instructions to the WebSurfer, often with slight modifications, but the WebSurfer continues to fail or provide redundant information.
  - **Metadata Extraction:** The agent extracts metadata or introductory text from the web page, but fails to provide the requested detailed content or specific information.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics
- **Specific Questions:**
  - **Has the web page fully loaded?** Ask the WebSurfer to confirm if the web page has fully loaded and if there are any issues with the page content.
  - **What is the exact error message?** Request the WebSurfer to provide the exact error message encountered during the loading attempt.
  - **Can you load a different source?** Instruct the WebSurfer to try loading a different, but equally reputable source to see if the issue is specific to the problematic page.

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Content Completeness Check:** Evaluate the completeness of the content provided by the WebSurfer. If the content is consistently partial or introductory, it may indicate a loading issue.
  - **Source Diversity Check:** Ensure that the WebSurfer is able to access multiple sources. If the issue is with a single source, it may be a specific problem rather than a general error.
  - **Conclusion Consistency Check:** Compare the conclusions drawn by the Planner across multiple interactions. Inconsistent conclusions, especially when the same source is cited, can indicate a problem with the information being gathered.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - **"Timeout 60000ms exceeded."** This phrase indicates a timeout error during page loading.
  - **"The web browser is open to the page [URL]."** This phrase, followed by partial content or metadata, suggests that the page is not fully loading.
  - **"I typed [URL] into the browser address bar."** This phrase, repeated multiple times, indicates that the agent is attempting to load the same page repeatedly.
  - **"No prior results to summarize yet."** This phrase from the Planner suggests that the agent is starting a new search, but if it is repeated without new information, it may indicate a failure to gather new data.
  - **"Provide a brief synthesized note."** If the WebSurfer fails to provide the requested synthesized note and instead provides redundant or partial content, it may indicate an error in content extraction.

### Example Application of the Template
- **Identify Repetitive Actions:** Notice if the WebSurfer is repeatedly trying to load the same page or perform the same search.
- **Check for Timeout Messages:** Look for timeout error messages in the WebSurfer's responses.
- **Evaluate Content Completeness:** If the content provided by the WebSurfer is consistently partial or only introductory, it suggests a loading issue.

  - **Overemphasis on Environmental Factors:** The agent emphasizes environmental factors (e.g., crown position, spacing treatment) without adequately exploring genetic factors or their relative importance.

- **Contextual Conditions:**

- **Sequence of Interactions:**
  - **Initial Lack of Specificity:** The initial search queries are not specific enough to find the required evidence.
  - **Repetitive Queries:** The agent repeats similar search queries without varying the approach or keywords to find more relevant information.
  - **Delayed Recognition of Need for Specific Evidence:** The agent recognizes the need for specific evidence only after multiple rounds of similar, non-conclusive results.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions:**
  - **"What specific evidence do we have that directly addresses the heritability of needle length in Abies balsamea?"**

  - **Identify Key Findings:** Look for key findings that directly address the question, such as heritability estimates, genetic studies, or comparative analyses of genetic vs. environmental influences.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - **"We still need explicit evidence"** or **"No definitive evidence yet"**: These phrases indicate that the agent is struggling to find relevant information and is not making progress.
  - **"Leans toward environmental/within-tree effects"**: This phrase suggests a bias towards environmental explanations without sufficient exploration of genetic factors.
  - **"Rephrasing the question"**: If the agent rephrases the question in a way that avoids the core issue, it may indicate an error in understanding or a failure to find relevant information.
  - **"Prioritize credible sources"**: Repeated emphasis on prioritizing credible sources without actually finding or citing them can signal a lack of progress in the research.

By using this template, future conversations can be analyzed to quickly identify when an agent is stuck in a loop of rephrasing and emphasizing environmental factors without addressing the core genetic question, leading to a more efficient error detection process.

  - "No prior specialist output yet" mentioned in the analysis, indicating a lack of preliminary information.
  - "Remember to: 1. Break down the task into manageable steps 2. Gather information systematically 3. Build upon previous results" is ignored or not followed in the response.

This template is designed to help identify when a planner in a multi-agent conversation system might be making an error due to a misunderstanding of basic physical concepts, particularly in scenarios involving phase changes of substances in controlled environments like freezers.

Generated Schema:
### Error Schema for Identifying Similar Errors in Multi-Agent Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Repetitive Search Queries:** The agent repeatedly suggests the same or very similar search queries, indicating a lack of progression in information gathering.
- **Lack of Concrete Evidence:** The agent's responses often lack direct, concrete evidence or specific data points that would help validate the hypothesis or answer.
- **Focus on Incorrect Sources:** The agent repeatedly returns to sources that are either not authoritative or do not directly address the question, such as general search engine result pages or educational materials without empirical data.
- **Inconsistent or Unchanged Summaries:** The summaries provided by the agent do not change significantly over multiple iterations, suggesting that new searches are not yielding new or more relevant information.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Task Complexity:** The task involves a complex question requiring specific, region-focused data and authoritative sources.
- **Initial Hypothesis:** The initial hypothesis is based on general scientific knowledge, but the agent fails to substantiate it with region-specific, empirical evidence.
- **Sequence of Interactions:**
  - **Initial Breakdown:** The agent correctly breaks down the task and identifies the need for authoritative sources.
  - **First Search Attempt:** The agent conducts a search but returns low-reliability results.
  - **Subsequent Search Attempts:** The agent continues to suggest similar search queries or sources that do not provide the necessary specific, region-focused data.
  - **Persistent Lack of Progress:** Despite multiple iterations, the agent does not move closer to a definitive answer supported by concrete evidence.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Questions to Determine Error Presence:**
  - **Are the search queries changing or becoming more specific over time?**
  - **Do the sources provided contain empirical data or direct, region-specific evidence?**
  - **Is the agent returning to the same types of sources (e.g., general search pages, educational materials) without finding new, authoritative data?**
  - **Are the summaries provided by the agent consistent across iterations without adding new, relevant information?**

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Evaluate Search Query Evolution:** Track the evolution of search queries to see if they become more targeted and specific with each iteration.
  - **Assess Source Authority:** Determine if the sources provided are from recognized authoritative bodies (e.g., EPA, USGS, NOAA) or if they are general, low-reliability sources.
  - **Check for Empirical Data:** Ensure that the sources contain empirical data, such as measurements, studies, or reports, that directly address the question.
  - **Monitor Information Gain:** Evaluate whether the information provided by the agent is incrementally building towards a definitive answer or if it remains static and non-specific.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - **"No more results found"** or **"No concrete results yet"**: Indicates the agent is not finding relevant information.
  - **"Retry search without quotes"**: Suggests the agent is struggling to find specific, authoritative sources.
  - **"For this study, lakes with a pH of 5.5 or lower were considered acid"**: While relevant, this phrase indicates a general description rather than a specific link to the cause of acidification.
  - **"The United States and Canada begin study of cross-border acid rain transport"**: Points to general studies without concrete, region-specific findings.
  - **"EPA-focused results"**: Indicates a bias towards EPA sources without exploring other authoritative bodies like USGS or NOAA.
  - **"Include any discussion of hydroelectric facilities or reservoirs only if relevant"**: This phrase is repeated without the agent finding relevant information, suggesting a failure to identify or exclude non-relevant discussions.

### Example Application of the Template:
- **Error Signature:** The WebSurfer agent repeatedly suggests the same search queries and returns to general search engine result pages without finding specific, authoritative sources.
- **Error Context Analysis:** The task involves a complex environmental question requiring region-specific data. The agent's initial hypothesis is correct, but the subsequent searches fail to gather the necessary empirical evidence.
- **Detection Heuristic:** 
  - **Evaluate Search Query Evolution:** The search queries are not becoming more specific or targeted.
  - **Assess Source Authority:** The sources are primarily from EPA, but they lack the region-specific, empirical data needed to validate the hypothesis.
  - **Check for Empirical Data:** The summaries provided do not contain concrete data or direct quotes from studies that link coal-fired power plants to lake acidification in the Northeast.
  - **Monitor Information Gain:** The information provided remains general and does not build towards a definitive answer.

By applying this template, future conversations can be analyzed to quickly identify when an agent is failing to gather the necessary specific, authoritative evidence to support a hypothesis or answer a complex question.

### Error Schema for Error Identification in Multi-Agent Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Distinctive Patterns or Signals:**
  - **Repetitive Querying:** The agent repeatedly queries the same search engine or dataset page without extracting specific, required data.
  - **Lack of Data Extraction:** The agent reports opening a webpage or dataset but fails to extract and report the specific numerical data requested.
  - **Metadata Reporting Instead of Content:** The agent provides metadata of the webpage (e.g., meta tags, viewport information) instead of the content or data that was the focus of the task.
  - **Partial Information Reporting:** The agent reports partial information, such as the existence of a file or dataset, but does not complete the task of extracting the required data.
  - **File Not Found Errors:** The agent reports a `FileNotFoundError` when attempting to read a file that was previously identified as existing and relevant.

- **Telltale Signs in Agent Behavior or Responses:**
  - **Rephrasing of Queries:** The agent rephrases the search query multiple times without a change in the outcome.
  - **Repeated URL Opening:** The agent opens the same URL multiple times without providing new or specific data.
  - **Focus on Non-Data Elements:** The agent focuses on non-relevant elements of the webpage, such as navigation menus, search settings, or promotional content.
  - **Inability to Navigate to Specific Data:** The agent navigates to the correct webpage but does not seem to know how to locate or download the specific file or data required.

=== Schema for Error Log 123 ===
Generated Schema:
### Error Schema for Error Identification in Multi-Agent Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Distinctive Patterns or Signals:**
  - The agent jumps to a conclusion without breaking down the task into manageable steps or gathering necessary information.
  - The response is overly specific or detailed, suggesting a deep dive into the topic without a preliminary exploration.
  - The agent provides an answer that is logically consistent but lacks empirical or scientific backing.
  - The agent incorrectly prioritizes one piece of information over others, leading to a misinformed decision.

- **Telltale Signs in the Agent's Behavior or Responses:**
  - Immediate assertion of a final answer without consulting available capabilities or historical data.
  - Use of advanced genetic or biological concepts without verifying the basic understanding or relevance to the question.
  - Overconfidence in the answer, indicating a lack of doubt or need for further investigation.
  - Failure to acknowledge the possibility of other influencing factors or the need for more data.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Contextual Conditions:**
  - The task involves a question that requires a factual or scientific explanation.
  - The available capabilities include tools for gathering information (e.g., WebSurfer, FileSurfer), but these are not utilized.
  - The conversation is at the initial stage, and no prior analysis or data has been provided.

- **Sequence of Interactions:**
  - The user presents a clear, specific question.
  - The agent does not request additional information or clarification.
  - The agent does not engage any specialists for data collection or verification.
  - The agent provides a detailed, but potentially incorrect, explanation and final answer in the first step.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions:**
  - Did the agent break down the task into smaller steps before providing an answer?
  - Did the agent use any of the available capabilities to gather information or verify facts?
  - Is the agent's response based on empirical data or scientific research?
  - Does the agent acknowledge the complexity of the topic or the need for more information?

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Step-by-Step Breakdown:** Check if the agent's response includes a step-by-step plan or if it directly jumps to a conclusion.
  - **Information Gathering:** Assess whether the agent has utilized or mentioned the need to use available tools for information gathering.
  - **Empirical Basis:** Evaluate if the agent's answer is supported by empirical evidence or scientific references.
  - **Complexity Recognition:** Determine if the agent has recognized the multifaceted nature of the question or simplified it too much.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - **Immediate Finality:** "The final answer is..." or "The deciding factor is..." used in the first response.
  - **Overconfidence:** Phrases like "Obviously," "Clearly," or "Without a doubt" that suggest the agent is certain without evidence.
  - **Advanced Terminology:** Use of terms such as "polygenic," "incomplete dominance," "variable expressivity," without a foundational step explaining these concepts.
  - **Lack of Questions:** Absence of follow-up questions or requests for more data, indicating a lack of critical thinking or thoroughness.
  - **Unverified Assertions:** Statements that are presented as facts without citation or verification, such as "Hair curliness is a genetic trait with incomplete dominance and variable expressivity."

This template can be used to quickly identify when an agent is making a premature and potentially incorrect decision in a conversation, especially in tasks that require factual or scientific accuracy.

### Error Schema for Identifying Similar Errors in Future Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Distinctive Patterns or Signals:**
  - Incorrect application of basic physics or chemistry principles.
  - Confusion between the concepts of charge and mass.
  - Misinterpretation of the relationship between the number of protons and electrons in an ion.
  - Incorrect calculation or reasoning involving the net charge of an ion.
- **Telltale Signs in the Agent's Behavior or Responses:**
  - Providing a final answer without a clear, step-by-step reasoning process.
  - Jumping to a conclusion based on a misunderstanding of fundamental concepts.
  - Failing to recognize the distinction between charge and mass, leading to incorrect choices.
  - Errors in basic arithmetic or algebra when calculating the net charge.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Contextual Conditions:**
  - The task involves a multiple-choice question in chemistry or physics.
  - The question specifically asks about the properties of ions, particularly their charge or mass.
  - The question provides a direct relationship between the number of protons and electrons (e.g., "three more electrons than protons").
- **Sequence of Interactions:**
  - The Planner agent is engaged first to analyze the task.
  - The Planner agent attempts to solve the problem without consulting additional resources or capabilities.
  - The Planner agent provides a final answer quickly, without detailed verification or consideration of the concepts involved.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions to Determine if the Error is Present:**
  - "What is the difference between the charge and mass of an ion?"
  - "How do you calculate the net charge of an ion given the number of protons and electrons?"
  - "Can you explain the relationship between the number of protons and electrons in this ion?"
  - "What is the charge of an electron and a proton?"
- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Concept Verification:**
    - Check if the agent correctly identifies the charge of an electron and a proton.
    - Verify if the agent understands that the net charge is the difference between the number of protons and electrons.
  - **Calculation Review:**
    - Review the agent's arithmetic or algebraic steps to ensure they are correctly applied.
    - Confirm if the agent has considered the correct physical properties (charge vs. mass) in their calculations.
  - **Resource Utilization:**
    - Assess whether the agent has used available resources (e.g., WebSurfer, FileSurfer) to verify their understanding or calculations.
    - Determine if the agent has skipped steps that could have helped in double-checking the answer.
- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - "Mass is not determined by the sign of the charge."
  - "Net charge = (+1)e·N_p + (-1)e·N_e"
  - "The ion has a charge of +3."
  - "The ion has a mass of +3." or "The ion has a mass of -3."
  - "This is a straightforward question."
  - "No prior result exists."
  - "TASK_COMPLETE" or "Final Answer" without detailed reasoning or verification steps.

### Template Usage:
- **Review Agent Responses:**
  - Look for quick, conclusive answers that lack detailed reasoning.
  - Identify any confusion between charge and mass.
  - Check for incorrect application of fundamental physics or chemistry principles.
- **Ask Probing Questions:**
  - Use the specific questions listed above to probe the agent's understanding and reasoning.
- **Analyze Interaction Patterns:**
  - Examine the sequence of interactions to see if the Planner agent has acted alone without consulting other resources.
  - Look for key phrases that indicate a lack of thorough analysis or a rush to a conclusion.

This template will help in recognizing when a similar error might be occurring in future conversations, particularly in scenarios involving basic scientific principles and calculations.

### Error Schema for Identifying Similar Errors in Future Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Misinterpretation of Question Requirements:**
  - The agent selects an answer that is logically opposite to the correct one, based on a misinterpretation of the question's intent (e.g., choosing a behavior that wild animals are more likely to exhibit when asked about tame animals).
- **Premature Conclusion:**
  - The agent concludes the task without engaging in any information gathering or verification steps, relying solely on initial assumptions.
- **Inconsistent Logical Reasoning:**
  - The agent's reasoning process is flawed, often comparing options inaccurately or not considering all given options equally.
- **Ignoring Specific Instructions:**
  - The agent bypasses specific instructions provided in the task, such as breaking down the task into steps or gathering information systematically.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Task Type:**
  - The task is a multiple-choice question that requires distinguishing between two categories (tame vs. wild animals).
- **Initial Response:**
  - The agent provides an immediate answer without any preliminary analysis or engagement of available capabilities.
- **Lack of Step-by-Step Breakdown:**
  - The agent does not break down the task into smaller, manageable steps as instructed.
- **Context Ignorance:**
  - The agent's response shows a lack of consideration for the context provided in the task, such as the nature of tame and wild animals and their typical behaviors.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions:**
  - "Did the agent engage any of the available capabilities to gather information before providing an answer?"
  - "Does the agent's response align with the question's intent to identify a behavior that is more characteristic of tame animals?"
  - "Did the agent consider all options equally in their reasoning process?"
  - "Is the agent's conclusion based on a logical interpretation of the differences between tame and wild animals?"
- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Step Verification:** Check if the agent followed the step-by-step approach outlined in the task.
  - **Category Analysis:** Evaluate whether the agent correctly identified the category (tame animals) and compared the behaviors accordingly.
  - **Option Assessment:** Ensure that the agent has assessed each option logically and provided a rationale for why the chosen answer is the most appropriate.
  - **Consistency Check:** Verify if the agent's reasoning is consistent and free from logical contradictions.
- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - "No prior result exists for this task."
  - "This is a straightforward knowledge question."
  - "The behavior that would most clearly differentiate [one category] from [another category] is [incorrect option]."
  - "TASK_COMPLETE" or "Final Answer:" without any intermediate steps or use of capabilities.
  - "The other options are assessed by how distinctly they separate the groups," followed by a selection that does not actually fit the criteria.

### Template Application:
When reviewing future conversations, apply the above heuristics to identify if the agent is making a similar error:
1. **Look for Misinterpretation:**
   - If the agent selects an option that is more characteristic of the opposite category, it may indicate a misunderstanding of the task.
2. **Check for Premature Conclusion:**
   - If the agent provides a final answer immediately without any steps or use of capabilities, it suggests a premature conclusion.
3. **Evaluate Logical Reasoning:**
   - If the agent's reasoning is inconsistent or illogical, particularly in comparing behaviors between categories, it may be an error.
4. **Verify Instruction Adherence:**
   - If the agent ignores the instructions to break down the task or gather information systematically, it could lead to incorrect answers.

=== Schema for Error Log 126 ===
Generated Schema:
### Error Schema for Identifying Comparative Advantage Misinterpretation Errors in Multi-Agent Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Distinctive Patterns or Signals:**
  - The agent selects an option that is not unique to the subject (e.g., plants) when the question asks for a comparative advantage.
  - The agent fails to consider the relative nature of the options provided, leading to a choice that is advantageous but not necessarily more so than the other options for the subject in question.
  - The agent overlooks the specific context (e.g., drought) and chooses an answer that is generally true but not context-specific.

- **Telltale Signs in the Agent's Behavior or Responses:**
  - The response includes a justification that does not explicitly compare the subject (e.g., plants) to the comparator (e.g., animals).
  - The agent mentions general traits or abilities of the subject that are not directly related to the context of the question.
  - The agent's reasoning is based on absolute statements rather than relative advantages (e.g., "Plants can live without water" instead of "Plants can survive with less water than animals").

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Typical Contextual Conditions:**
  - The question is framed in a comparative manner, asking for an advantage of one entity over another.
  - The options provided include both unique and general traits of the subject.
  - The context of the question is specific (e.g., drought, extreme cold), but the chosen answer does not reflect this specificity.

- **Sequence of Interactions:**
  - The conversation starts with a clear, comparative question.
  - The Planner or a similar decision-making agent is the first to respond, often without prior research or analysis.
  - The agent's response is immediate and based on a general understanding of the subject rather than a detailed or context-specific analysis.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions to Determine Error Presence:**
  - Does the selected answer clearly state a comparative advantage of the subject over the comparator?
  - Is the reasoning provided based on the specific context of the question?
  - Are there any absolute statements in the justification that do not hold up to scrutiny in the given context?

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Comparative Analysis:** Ensure that the agent's response addresses the relative nature of the advantage (i.e., how the subject's trait or ability compares to the comparator's in the given context).
  - **Context Specificity:** Verify that the reasoning is tightly linked to the specific conditions mentioned in the question (e.g., drought, temperature extremes).
  - **Trait Uniqueness:** Check if the chosen trait or ability is unique to the subject or if it can also apply to the comparator, possibly with modifications.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - **Lack of Comparative Language:** Phrases like "Plants can..." or "Plants have..." without mentioning "more than" or "better than" animals.
  - **Generalizations:** Statements that are too broad, such as "Plants can live without water" or "Plants release oxygen."
  - **Absolute Terms:** Use of absolute terms like "all," "never," or "always" in the justification, which may not be accurate in a comparative context.
  - **Ignoring Context:** Responses that do not reference the specific environmental condition (e.g., drought) or its implications on the subject and comparator.

By using this template, future conversations can be monitored for similar misinterpretations of comparative advantage, ensuring that the analysis and decision-making process are aligned with the specific requirements of the question.

### Error Schema for Identifying Similar Errors in Future Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:

  - The agent provides a definitive answer that contradicts basic ecological principles or the information given in the question.
  - The agent makes assumptions or predictions about outcomes without considering the full range of possible ecological factors or behaviors.
  - The agent overlooks the direct relationship between the pollinator and the plant species, leading to an incorrect prediction about plant population dynamics.
  - The agent's response does not align with the logical implications of the decrease in a specific pollinator population.

- **Telltale Signs in Agent's Behavior or Responses:**
  - The agent jumps to conclusions without a thorough analysis of the scenario.
  - The agent fails to break down the task into logical steps or sub-tasks.
  - The agent does not mention or consider alternative pollinators or ecological buffers.
  - The agent does not use the available capabilities to gather additional information or validate assumptions.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Contextual Conditions:**
  - The question involves a scenario where there is a clear dependency between two species (e.g., plant and pollinator).
  - The scenario includes a change in the population of one species, and the task is to predict the impact on the other.
  - The agent is expected to make a prediction based on the given information without requiring additional data.
  - The agent is working on a task for the first time and has no previous results to build upon.

- **Sequence of Interactions:**
  - The user presents a hypothetical ecological scenario.
  - The agent (Planner) is asked to analyze the task and decide on the next steps.
  - The agent provides a final answer without engaging in further analysis or data gathering.
  - The agent's decision-making process is not transparent, and key ecological concepts are not discussed.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions to Determine Error Presence:**
  - "Does the agent's prediction consider all the ecological factors mentioned in the question?"
  - "Does the agent acknowledge the direct relationship between the pollinator and the plant species?"
  - "Has the agent used the available capabilities to gather more information or validate the assumptions?"
  - "Does the agent's response logically follow from the given scenario?"

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Step-by-Step Verification:**
    - Identify the key relationships and dependencies in the scenario (e.g., blue flowers and bees, white flowers and moths).
    - Check if the agent's response considers the immediate and direct consequences of the change in the pollinator population.
    - Evaluate whether the agent has considered alternative ecological factors that could influence the outcome (e.g., other pollinators, plant reproduction methods).
    - Assess if the agent's reasoning is transparent and logically sound based on the provided information.
  - **Consistency with Ecological Principles:**
    - Determine if the agent's answer is consistent with known ecological principles, such as the impact of pollinator decline on plant populations.
    - Verify if the agent has correctly interpreted the role of the pollinator in the plant's life cycle and population dynamics.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - "The population of [species] will [increase/decrease/remain unchanged] over time."
  - "Without quantifying [factor], a net [increase/decrease] is not guaranteed."
  - "The [species] are not directly influenced by [change in another species]; the least-assumption, most direct prediction is no change."
  - "The [species] will start [new behavior] due to [change in another species]."
  - "The scenario suggests that [species] are [dependent/independent] on [pollinator/another factor], and the agent's response does not reflect this dependency."

### Application of the Template
- **When to Apply:**
  - Use this template when the conversation involves ecological scenarios with clear species dependencies.
  - Apply after the agent provides a response to ensure the prediction is logical and comprehensive.
  - Utilize during the initial analysis phase to catch early signs of incorrect assumptions or conclusions.

- **How to Apply:**
  - Review the agent's response for the presence of the key phrases listed above.
  - Ask the specific questions outlined to probe the agent's reasoning and assumptions.
  - Use the analytical framework to systematically verify each step of the agent's decision-making process.

This template is designed to help identify when an agent makes incorrect predictions in ecological scenarios due to oversimplification or overlooking key relationships and factors.

### Error Schema for Identifying Similar Errors in Future Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Misapplication of Ohm's Law:** The agent incorrectly applies Ohm's Law, stating \( I = \frac{R}{V} \) instead of \( I = \frac{V}{R} \).
- **Incorrect Mathematical Transformation:** The agent performs an incorrect mathematical transformation when analyzing the effect of doubling the resistance, leading to a conclusion that the current will also double.
- **Confusion of Proportional Relationships:** The agent confuses the inverse relationship between current and resistance in a circuit with a direct proportional relationship.
- **Premature Task Completion:** The agent declares the task complete without verifying the correctness of the answer or consulting other specialists.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Contextual Conditions:**
  - The task involves a basic physics concept, specifically Ohm's Law.
  - The question requires understanding the relationship between voltage, current, and resistance.
  - The task is presented without any prior specialist results or additional context.
- **Preceding Interactions:**
  - The user provides a clear and specific question.
  - The Planner is the first to engage and analyze the task.
  - The Planner does not break down the task into detailed steps or consider using the WebSurfer or FileSurfer capabilities to verify the information.
  - The Planner does not consult the Coder or CodeExecutor to perform a calculation check.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions:**
  - "Can you confirm the correct form of Ohm's Law?"
  - "How does the current change when resistance is increased, assuming voltage remains constant?"
  - "Did you verify the mathematical transformation you applied to the equation?"
  - "Are there any other resources or specialists you could consult to ensure the accuracy of your response?"
- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Equation Verification:** Check if the agent correctly identifies and uses the equation \( I = \frac{V}{R} \).
  - **Proportional Relationship Analysis:** Assess whether the agent understands that current and resistance are inversely proportional when voltage is constant.
  - **Step-by-Step Breakdown:** Evaluate if the agent breaks down the problem into clear, logical steps and justifies each step.
  - **Cross-Verification:** Determine if the agent considers using other available capabilities (e.g., WebSurfer, Coder) to cross-verify their conclusions.
- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - "With constant voltage V, current I = R/V (Ohm’s Law)."
  - "Increasing R to 2R makes I become (2R)/V, which is twice the original current."
  - "No prior specialist results exist."
  - "This is a straightforward physics question."
  - "TASK_COMPLETE"
  - "Final Answer: The current will be doubled (Option B)."

### Template Summary
- **Signatures:** Misapplication of Ohm's Law, incorrect mathematical transformations, confusion of proportional relationships, and premature task completion.
- **Context:** Basic physics questions involving Ohm's Law, where the relationship between voltage, current, and resistance is crucial.
- **Heuristics:** Verify the form of the equation used, analyze the understanding of inverse proportional relationships, check for a step-by-step problem breakdown, and look for cross-verification with other resources or specialists. Key phrases often indicate a lack of thorough analysis and a rush to conclude the task.

  - **"The most direct benefit is..."** without further exploration of other potential benefits.

### Error Schema for Identifying Premature Hypothesis Rejection Errors

#### 1. Error Signatures
- **Overconfidence in New Evidence:** The agent shows a high level of confidence in the new evidence without considering the need for further verification or the possibility of integrating it with existing theories.
- **Immediate Conclusion Drawing:** The agent jumps to a conclusion that the old hypothesis will be completely discarded and replaced by the new one without suggesting intermediate steps such as verification, testing, or revision.
- **Lack of Scientific Process Acknowledgment:** The agent does not mention or consider the scientific process, which typically involves peer review, experimental testing, and gradual acceptance or rejection of hypotheses based on cumulative evidence.
- **Misapplication of Philosophical Rules:** The agent incorrectly applies philosophical rules of science, such as Popperian falsification, to justify the immediate rejection of the old hypothesis.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis
- **Scientific Inquiry Task:** The task involves evaluating the impact of new scientific evidence on existing hypotheses or theories.
- **New vs. Old Evidence:** The context provides new evidence that contradicts or challenges a well-established scientific hypothesis.
- **Decision-Making Step:** The error occurs during the initial decision-making step where the agent is supposed to break down the task and consider appropriate actions.
- **Prior Knowledge Influence:** The agent's response is influenced by a simplified understanding of scientific methodology, leading to an overly decisive action.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics
- **Questions to Ask:**
  - "Is the new evidence thoroughly verified and tested?"
  - "What is the current scientific consensus on the new evidence?"
  - "How does the scientific community typically handle new evidence that contradicts existing theories?"
  - "Is there a possibility that the old hypothesis can be revised or integrated with the new evidence?"
  - "What steps are usually taken before a hypothesis is considered obsolete or replaced?"
- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Scientific Methodology:** Evaluate whether the agent's response aligns with the standard steps of the scientific method, including hypothesis testing, peer review, and gradual acceptance.
  - **Evidence Verification:** Assess if the agent considers the need for verification and further testing of the new evidence before making a conclusion.
  - **Theory Integration Potential:** Determine if the agent explores the possibility of integrating or revising the old hypothesis rather than outright discarding it.
  - **Philosophical Consistency:** Check if the agent correctly applies philosophical principles of science, such as falsifiability, to justify their conclusion.
- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - "The old hypothesis will be discarded and replaced."
  - "The new evidence is conclusive."
  - "There is no need to verify the new evidence."
  - "The scientific community will immediately accept the new hypothesis."
  - "The prior theory is rejected due to the new evidence."
  - "The posterior for the new model exceeds 0.5, warranting immediate replacement."

This template is designed to help identify when an agent is making a premature decision to reject an old hypothesis based on new evidence, without considering the necessary scientific steps and processes.

  - Relying on implied coefficients without explicitly stating or verifying them.
  - Jumping to a conclusion based on a superficial inspection of the options.

  - The agent makes a decision without engaging with available specialists or performing additional checks.
- **Sequence of Interactions:**
  - The conversation starts with a clear question and options.
  - The Planner immediately provides an analysis and a final answer.
  - There is no prior interaction with other specialists, such as the Coder or CodeExecutor, to verify the balance of the equations.
  - The response is given in a single step without iterative refinement or checking.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions to Determine if the Error is Present:**
  - "Can you break down each equation step by step and count the atoms of each element on both sides?"
  - "Are you sure that the equation is balanced without adding any coefficients?"
  - "What are the coefficients needed for each equation to be balanced?"
  - "How did you verify that the equation demonstrates the conservation of mass?"
- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Step-by-Step Verification:**
    - Identify the elements in each equation.
    - Count the number of atoms of each element on the reactants side.
    - Count the number of atoms of each element on the products side.
    - Compare the counts to determine if the equation is balanced.
  - **Principle Application:**
    - Ensure the agent is applying the principle of conservation of mass correctly.
    - Check if the agent is considering the need for coefficients to balance the equation.
  - **Consistency Check:**
    - Verify if the agent’s response aligns with the counts and principles.
    - Look for any logical inconsistencies in the agent’s explanation.
- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - "No previous specialist output exists for this task."
  - "Therefore, the correctly written equation is..."
  - "Brief explanation: The equation demonstrates conservation with implied coefficients..."
  - "TASK_COMPLETE" followed by a quick final answer without detailed verification.

### Example Usage:
- **Identify the Elements and Atom Counts:**
  - "Let's start by identifying the elements and counting the atoms in each equation."
- **Check for Coefficients:**
  - "Do any of these equations require coefficients to be balanced? Can you show me how you would balance them?"
- **Compare Atom Counts:**
  - "Now, let's compare the atom counts on both sides of each equation to ensure they are balanced."
- **Verify Logical Consistency:**
  - "Can you explain how you verified that the equation demonstrates the conservation of mass?"

### Error Schema for Identifying Premature Task Completion Errors in Multi-Agent Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Premature Conclusion:** The agent reaches a conclusion without performing any intermediate steps or analysis.
- **Lack of Engagement with Task Requirements:** The agent does not engage with the requirement to "analyze the task and decide which specialist to engage first."
- **Incorrect Logical Reasoning:** The agent provides a final answer based on an incorrect or incomplete understanding of the historical context or scientific progression of the statements.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Initial Task Context:** The task involves identifying a statement that has changed due to new scientific information.
- **Agent's Initial Interaction:** The agent's first action is to provide a final answer without any preliminary steps.
- **Absence of Information Gathering:** There is no indication of the agent using any of the available capabilities (WebSurfer, FileSurfer, Coder, CodeExecutor) to gather or analyze information.
- **Sequence of Interactions:** The error typically occurs in the first step of the conversation, immediately after the user presents the task.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions to Determine Error Presence:**
  - "Did the agent use any of the available capabilities to gather information before providing an answer?"
  - "Was the agent's response based on a detailed analysis of the historical context of each statement?"
  - "Did the agent explain the reasoning process for how it arrived at its conclusion?"

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Step-by-Step Analysis:** Check if the agent has broken down the task into smaller, manageable steps.
  - **Use of Capabilities:** Verify if the agent has utilized the available tools (WebSurfer, FileSurfer, Coder, CodeExecutor) to support its reasoning.
  - **Historical and Scientific Context:** Evaluate if the agent's response aligns with the historical and scientific context of the statements provided.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - Phrases indicating immediate finality: "The answer is," "Final Answer," "TASK_COMPLETE."
  - Lack of questions or requests for information: The agent does not ask for more data or suggest using any capabilities.
  - Absence of analysis: The response lacks any discussion of how the conclusion was reached, including no mention of historical evidence or scientific progression.
  - Overly confident statements without supporting evidence: The agent provides a definitive answer without acknowledging the complexity or need for further investigation.

### Application Example:
**User Input:**
Scientists often change conclusions when new information becomes available. Which statement was changed because new information became available? A) The Moon is in outer space., B) Plants make their own food., C) The Sun revolves around Earth., D) Paper products come from wood.

**Agent Response:**
The statement that changed due to new information is B) Plants make their own food. Historically, the acceptance of photosynthesis emerged from new experimental evidence (e.g., van Helmont, Priestley, Ingenhousz) that established how plants synthesize food from light, water, and air. The other statements (Moon in outer space, the Sun revolves around Earth, paper from wood) reflect either straightforward observations, are presently false, or did not hinge on discovery-driven acceptance due to new information.

**Using the Template:**
- **Error Signatures:** The agent provided a final answer without breaking down the task or engaging any capabilities.
- **Error Context Analysis:** The task was to identify a statement that has changed due to new scientific information, and the agent's response was given in the first step without any preliminary analysis.
- **Detection Heuristics:**
  - **Step-by-Step Analysis:** The agent did not break down the task.
  - **Use of Capabilities:** The agent did not use any available tools.
  - **Historical and Scientific Context:** The agent's response was based on a misunderstanding of the historical context. The correct answer is C) The Sun revolves around Earth, which was changed from the geocentric to the heliocentric model due to new astronomical evidence.
  - **Key Phrases:** The agent used phrases like "Final Answer" and "TASK_COMPLETE" without engaging in the required analysis process.

This template can be used to systematically identify when an agent prematurely completes a task without thorough analysis, particularly in contexts where historical or scientific progression is a critical factor in determining the correct answer.

- **Lack of Analysis Depth:** The response shows a minimal or cursory analysis, indicating that the agent did not delve deeply into the specifics of the task or consider the complexity of the question.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:

- **Sequence of Interactions:** The error typically occurs when the planner is the first to respond and immediately concludes the task without breaking it down into steps or utilizing any of the available specialists.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:

  - Is the final answer provided in the first step of the conversation?

  - Are there any references to prior results or data that the agent should have gathered?
  - Does the agent's decision-making process seem rushed or incomplete?

  - **Step Count:** Check if the task was completed in fewer steps than expected for a complex question.
  - **Specialist Utilization:** Verify if any of the available specialists (WebSurfer, FileSurfer, Coder, CodeExecutor) were used in the conversation.
  - **Response Content Analysis:** Evaluate the content of the response for depth and accuracy. Look for:

    - Use of specific terms or concepts related to the task
    - Acknowledgment of the need for further information or verification
  - **Context Recognition:** Assess whether the planner recognized the context and the complexity of the task from the initial prompt.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**

    - "No prior specialist output yet. This is the first action."
    - "TASK_COMPLETE"

    - Absence of phrases like "Let's use WebSurfer to find more information" or "I will ask Coder to help with this."
  - **Superficial Analysis:**
    - Short, conclusory statements without detailed reasoning or evidence.
    - Phrases indicating a quick decision, such as "Clearly, the answer is [Option]" or "It’s obvious that [Option] is correct."

- **How to Use:** Use the detection heuristics to systematically review the planner's initial response and the overall conversation flow. Look for the presence of key phrases and the absence of specialist engagement to confirm the error.

This template is designed to help identify when a planner prematurely completes a task without adequate information or analysis, ensuring that such errors can be recognized early in future conversations.

=== Schema for Error Log 150 ===
Generated Schema:
### Error Schema for Identifying Conceptual Misalignment in Explanatory Responses

#### 1. Error Signatures
- **Distinctive Patterns or Signals:**
  - The agent selects an option that is closely related to the correct answer but does not fully capture the primary mechanism or reason.
  - The explanation provided is based on a secondary or contributory factor rather than the main causal factor.
  - The response may include accurate information but fails to distinguish between a descriptive and an explanatory answer.
- **Telltale Signs in Agent's Behavior or Responses:**
  - The agent's reasoning involves a concept that is a property or characteristic of the material/object but does not directly explain the phenomenon in question.
  - The agent's explanation might be logically sound but overly focuses on a less relevant aspect of the question.
  - The agent may use terms that are synonymous or closely related to the correct answer, leading to a subtle but significant misinterpretation of the question.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis
- **Contextual Conditions:**
  - The task involves selecting the best explanation from a list of options, where each option is a property or characteristic of the material/object.
  - The question is about a natural phenomenon or a scientific principle, requiring a clear understanding of the underlying cause.
  - The agent is expected to provide a reasoning step that justifies the selection of the answer.
- **Sequence of Interactions:**
  - The agent is asked to analyze a task and decide on the best course of action or answer.
  - The agent responds with a detailed explanation, often including scientific principles or properties.
  - The agent's explanation is thorough but may not directly address the core of the question, leading to a choice that is technically correct but not the best explanatory option.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics
- **Specific Questions:**
  - Does the selected answer directly explain the phenomenon, or does it describe a characteristic that contributes to the phenomenon?
  - Is the explanation based on a fundamental principle, or is it a restatement of the observation?
  - What is the primary mechanism behind the phenomenon, and does the agent's chosen answer reflect this mechanism?
- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Relevance Check:** Evaluate whether the chosen answer and its explanation are directly relevant to the question's core.
  - **Causality Analysis:** Determine if the explanation provided is a cause or an effect. Focus on identifying the primary cause.
  - **Comparative Evaluation:** Compare the agent's chosen answer with other options to see if a more direct or fundamental explanation is available.
- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - Phrases indicating a secondary factor: "contributes to," "leads to," "results in," "because of."
  - Phrases that restate the observation: "is buoyant," "is floatable," "experiences buoyancy."
  - Patterns where the agent's explanation is detailed but misses the fundamental cause: providing multiple related properties without linking them to the primary reason.
  - Patterns of choosing an answer that is a property of the material/object without explicitly explaining how this property affects the phenomenon: "Wood is porous" without explaining the relationship to density and buoyancy.

This template is designed to help identify when an agent's response, while containing accurate information, does not fully capture the primary mechanism or reason behind a phenomenon, leading to a conceptual misalignment.

  - Simple multiple-choice format: The task is presented in a multiple-choice format, which might lead the agent to select an answer based on initial assumptions.
  - Scientific or conceptual question: The question involves scientific properties or concepts that require a deeper understanding or specific data to answer accurately.
  - Limited initial data: The task starts with no prior specialist output or additional information provided.

- **Sequence of Interactions:**
  - Direct question to the Planner: The user or another agent directly asks the Planner to make a decision without any preliminary research or data gathering.
  - Planner's first action is the final answer: The Planner provides the final answer as its first action, bypassing the need for further analysis or consultation with other agents.

  - "Did you consider all the available options before deciding on the final answer?"
  - "What evidence do you have to support your choice?"
  - "How did you use the available capabilities to gather information?"
  - "Can you explain the reasoning behind each of the options before selecting the final answer?"

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Step-by-Step Breakdown:** Check if the agent has broken down the task into smaller, manageable steps.
  - **Information Gathering:** Assess whether the agent has used or planned to use any of the available capabilities (WebSurfer, FileSurfer, etc.) to gather relevant information.
  - **Comprehensive Analysis:** Evaluate if the agent has considered multiple aspects of the question and provided a well-rounded explanation.
  - **Validation and Verification:** Determine if the agent has sought to validate its answer through additional sources or by consulting other agents.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - "No prior specialist output."
  - "The task is a straightforward [subject] question."
  - "TASK_COMPLETE" or "Final Answer" in the first step or response.
  - "I think the answer is [option] because [brief reasoning]."
  - "I don't need to use [capability] for this."
  - "The most important property is [option], as it [general statement]."
