### Error Schema for Error Identification in Multi-Agent Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Incorrect Final Answer:** The agent provides an incorrect final answer that contradicts well-established scientific facts.
- **Misinterpretation of Options:** The agent misinterprets or confuses the given options, leading to a logically incorrect choice.
- **Overlooked Ground Truth:** The agent fails to correctly identify the ground truth or the correct answer among the options provided.
- **Confusion in Properties:** The agent confuses properties of different states of matter (e.g., solid, liquid, gas).

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Type of Task:** The task involves a multiple-choice question where the options are clearly stated and require basic scientific knowledge to answer.
- **Initial State of Conversation:** The conversation starts with a straightforward question and no prior specialist tasks or information gathering steps.
- **Agent's Role:** The error occurs when the Planner is making the decision without consulting other specialists or performing additional checks.
- **Preceding Interactions:** The Planner's decision is made based on the initial question without breaking down the task into steps or gathering information systematically.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions:**
  - "Does the final answer align with the basic properties of the states of matter mentioned in the options?"
  - "Has the agent correctly identified and distinguished between the properties of solids, liquids, and gases?"
  - "Is the chosen answer consistent with the ground truth or scientific facts?"

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Step-by-Step Verification:** Check if the agent has broken down the task into steps and verified the properties of each option.
  - **Fact-Checking:** Determine if the agent has used any fact-checking methods (like consulting WebSurfer or FileSurfer) to validate the options.
  - **Logical Consistency:** Assess whether the agent's reasoning is logically consistent with the properties of matter and the given options.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - "The correct statement is..." followed by an incorrect option.
  - "I decided to..." without mentioning any steps for breaking down the task or gathering information.
  - "Brief justification:" that provides a correct explanation for one option but incorrectly applies it to another.
  - "No prior specialist task completed" yet the agent proceeds to make a decision without further verification.
  - "Remember to: 1. Break down the task into manageable steps 2. Gather information systematically 3. Build upon previous results" followed by a response that ignores these instructions.

### Example Application of the Template
- **Conversation Review:**
  - **User Question:** "Which statement correctly describes a property of a type of matter? A) Air is a mixture of gases., B) Ice is a mixture of gases., C) Air is a liquid., D) Ice is a liquid."
  - **Planner Response:** "The correct statement is B) Ice is a mixture of gases. Brief justification: Air consists of multiple gases, making it a mixture; Ice is solid water, and neither Air nor Ice is a liquid in its standard form."

- **Error Identification:**
  - **Error Signature:** The final answer (B) is incorrect, and the justification is logically inconsistent.
  - **Context Analysis:** The task is a multiple-choice question, and the Planner made a decision without breaking it down or gathering additional information.
  - **Detection Heuristics:**
    - The answer "B) Ice is a mixture of gases" does not align with basic properties of matter.
    - The Planner's justification correctly describes air but incorrectly applies it to ice.
    - The response lacks a systematic approach to verifying the options, as indicated by the phrase "No prior specialist task completed" and the immediate decision without further steps.

This template can be used to systematically review and identify similar errors in future conversations where basic scientific facts are misinterpreted or where the agent fails to follow the structured approach outlined in the task instructions.

=== Schema for Error Log 153 ===
Generated Schema:
### Error Schema for Error Identification in Multi-Agent Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Misinterpretation of Frequency**:
  - **Patterns in Agent's Response**: The agent provides a frequency that is incorrect due to a misunderstanding of the context or specific conditions of the event. For example, stating that a daily event occurs once per year in a polar context.
  - **Distinctive Signals**: 
    - The agent mentions a frequency that is significantly lower or higher than expected based on general knowledge.
    - The agent's response includes a polar or extreme geographic condition that was not specified in the original question.

- **Inconsistent Data Handling**:
  - **Patterns in Agent's Response**: The agent mixes data from different contexts or locations without clearly distinguishing between them, leading to incorrect comparisons.
  - **Distinctive Signals**:
    - The agent combines frequency data from different regions (e.g., global vs. specific location) without noting the differences.
    - The agent fails to account for variations in frequency due to geographic or seasonal factors.

- **Premature Conclusion**:
  - **Patterns in Agent's Response**: The agent concludes the task without fully verifying all the necessary data points.
  - **Distinctive Signals**:
    - The agent states a final answer before all relevant frequencies have been gathered and compared.
    - The agent's conclusion is based on incomplete or partially verified data.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Contextual Conditions**:
  - **Question Type**: The question involves comparing the frequencies of different natural events, some of which have varying frequencies based on geographic location or other conditions.
  - **Available Information**: The conversation history shows that the agent has gathered some, but not all, of the necessary frequency data.
  - **Agent's Role**: The Planner is responsible for breaking down the task and guiding the WebSurfer to gather the required information. Errors often occur when the Planner misinterprets the data or draws conclusions too early.

- **Sequence of Interactions**:
  - **Initial Breakdown**: The Planner correctly breaks down the task and assigns the WebSurfer to gather data.
  - **Data Gathering**: The WebSurfer returns partial or context-specific data, which the Planner may not fully understand or interpret correctly.
  - **Premature Comparison**: The Planner attempts to compare the frequencies before all data is gathered or verified, leading to an incorrect conclusion.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions**:
  - **Frequency Verification**: "Is the frequency you provided for [event] consistent with the general understanding of its occurrence, or is it specific to a particular location or condition?"
  - **Data Completeness**: "Have we gathered and verified the frequency data for all the events mentioned in the question?"
  - **Contextual Consistency**: "Are the frequencies being compared in the same context (e.g., global, per location)?"

- **Analytical Framework**:
  - **Cross-Verification**: Ensure that the frequency data for each event is cross-verified with multiple authoritative sources.
  - **Contextual Analysis**: Analyze the context in which the frequency data is provided (e.g., polar, equatorial, global) and ensure it matches the context of the question.
  - **Step-by-Step Comparison**: Only draw conclusions after all relevant data points have been gathered and compared in a systematic manner.

- **Key Phrases and Conversation Patterns**:
  - **Misinterpretation Indicators**:
    - "approximately 1 time per year per location" when discussing a daily event.
    - "location-dependent" without further clarification or verification.
  - **Inconsistent Data Handling**:
    - "combining data from [source 1] and [source 2]" without noting differences in context.
    - "mixing global and local data" without clear distinction.
  - **Premature Conclusion**:
    - "Based on these frequencies, [event] is the most frequent" before all data is gathered.
    - "I will verify with sources to proceed confidently" followed by a conclusion without verification.
    - "TASK_COMPLETE" or "Final Answer" before all necessary information is provided and compared.

### Example Application:
- **Identify Misinterpretation**:
  - If an agent mentions that a daily event occurs once per year in a polar context, this is a clear misinterpretation.
- **Detect Inconsistent Data Handling**:
  - If an agent combines the frequency of a global event with a location-specific event without noting the differences, this indicates inconsistent data handling.
- **Recognize Premature Conclusion**:
  - If an agent states a final answer before all the required data has been gathered and verified, this is a sign of a premature conclusion.

By using this template, future conversations can be analyzed to quickly identify and recognize these types of errors, ensuring more accurate and reliable task completion.

  - The incorrect answer provided by the agent is a function related to the biological entity but is not the main function.

  - The user presents a clear, direct question.
  - The planner agent immediately responds with an answer without engaging in a step-by-step analysis or utilizing available capabilities.
  - The response is given as if the task is completed, without any indication of uncertainty or need for further research.

  - Is the agent's answer overly specific, focusing on a minor or secondary function rather than the main one?
  - Did the agent engage in a step-by-step analysis or utilize any available capabilities before providing the answer?

    - Clearly define what the biological entity is (e.g., photosynthetic cells).
    - Identify the primary function typically associated with this entity in biological processes.
  - **Step 2: Evaluate the Agent's Response**
    - Check if the response is a direct match to one of the options.
    - Determine if the response is too specific or focuses on a related but secondary function.
  - **Step 3: Cross-Verify with Known Facts**
    - Use a reliable source (e.g., a biology textbook, peer-reviewed article) to cross-verify the primary function of the biological entity.
    - Ensure that the agent's response aligns with these known facts.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - Phrases indicating a quick, definitive conclusion without evidence: "The main function is clearly...", "Correct option: [X] without hesitation."
  - Phrases suggesting a focus on a secondary function: "regulate the intake of carbon dioxide", "stomatal/guard-cell activity".
  - Phrases indicating a lack of systematic approach: "No prior result exists", "Remember to: [list of steps not followed]."
  - Immediate declaration of task completion: "TASK_COMPLETE", "Final Answer: [X]" without intermediate steps or analysis.

- **Telltale Signs in the Agent's Behavior or Responses:**
  - **Premature Conclusion:** The agent concludes the task without engaging in a deeper analysis or verification process.
  - **Lack of Comprehensive Evaluation:** The agent does not evaluate all given options thoroughly, dismissing some based on a single criterion.
  - **Confusion Between Operational and Definitional Differences:** The agent conflates operational characteristics with definitional ones, leading to a misalignment between the question's intent and the provided answer.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Contextual Conditions:**
  - **Knowledge-Based Questions:** The error occurs in the context of a question that requires specific knowledge about technical or scientific differences.
  - **Multiple Choice Format:** The question is presented in a multiple-choice format, which can sometimes lead to over-simplification or a focus on a single aspect of the options.
  - **First Interaction for the Task:** This type of error is more likely to occur during the first interaction, where the agent has no prior specialist results to build upon.

- **Sequence of Interactions:**
  - **Direct Response Without Initial Analysis:** The agent provides a direct response without breaking down the task into smaller, manageable steps or without gathering additional information.
  - **Lack of Step-by-Step Verification:** The agent does not systematically verify each option against known facts or definitions.
  - **Immediate Task Completion:** The agent marks the task as complete after a brief analysis, without considering the need for further clarification or deeper investigation.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions to Determine Error Presence:**
  - "Is the primary difference you identified also the most fundamental one in terms of the basic principles of the systems?"
  - "Have you considered all the options and their implications in the broader context of how these systems function?"
  - "Can you explain why the other options are incorrect, not just why your chosen option is correct?"

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Evaluate the Depth of Analysis:** Check if the agent's analysis covers all aspects of the question and options, or if it is shallow and focuses on a single, potentially less critical, difference.
  - **Cross-Check with Definitions:** Ensure that the agent's response aligns with the standard definitions and principles of the systems in question.
  - **Consider Practical vs Theoretical Differences:** Assess whether the agent is distinguishing between practical operational differences and theoretical definitions, and if the primary difference aligns with the question's intent.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - **"The primary difference is...":** This phrase can be a signal that the agent is making a definitive statement without sufficient analysis.
  - **"Other options are incorrect because...":** This often indicates that the agent is dismissing options based on a single criterion without a comprehensive evaluation.
  - **"This is a straightforward knowledge question":** This phrase might suggest that the agent is not planning to engage in a detailed analysis or verification process.
  - **"No prior specialist results":** This can indicate that the agent is making a decision based on limited information, which may lead to errors in complex or nuanced questions.

By using this template, future conversations can be analyzed for similar error patterns, helping to recognize when an agent might be focusing on secondary differences or oversimplifying complex concepts.

=== Schema for Error Log 158 ===
Generated Schema:
### Error Schema for Identifying Similar Errors in Future Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Misidentification of Variables:** The agent incorrectly identifies the dependent (measured) variable as the independent (manipulated) variable or vice versa.
- **Reversed Logic in Variable Roles:** The agent's reasoning suggests that the variable being manipulated is the one being measured or solved for, rather than recognizing that the manipulated variable is used to observe its effect on the measured variable.
- **Confusion Between Hypothesis and Variables:** The agent conflates the hypothesis (what is being tested) with the variables (factors being manipulated or measured).

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Task Involves Experiment Design:** The task typically involves an experimental setup where the goal is to determine the effect of one variable on another.
- **Incremental Adjustments in Trials:** The task often includes multiple trials where a specific variable (e.g., speed) is adjusted incrementally to observe changes in another variable (e.g., jump distance or success).
- **Agent's Initial Response:** The agent's initial response may show a clear misunderstanding of the experimental setup, particularly the roles of the variables involved.
- **Lack of Clarification:** The agent does not seek clarification on the roles of variables when the task description is ambiguous or when the user's question is not clearly understood.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Questions to Determine Error Presence:**
  - "What is the variable that the experimenter is changing in the trials?"
  - "What is the variable that the experimenter is measuring or observing as a result of the changes?"
  - "Can you explain the difference between the independent and dependent variables in this experiment?"
  - "What is the hypothesis that the experimenter is testing?"

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Identify the Manipulated Variable:** Determine which variable is being actively changed by the experimenter across different trials.
  - **Identify the Measured Variable:** Determine which variable is being observed or measured to see the effect of the manipulated variable.
  - **Check for Consistency:** Ensure that the agent's identification of variables is consistent with the experimental setup and the goal of the experiment.
  - **Hypothesis Verification:** Verify that the agent understands the hypothesis and can distinguish it from the variables being manipulated or measured.

- **Key Phrases and Conversation Patterns:**
  - **Phrases Indicating Misidentification:**
    - "The speed is the dependent variable because it is being solved for."
    - "The jump outcome is the independent variable because it guides the adjustments."
    - "The hypothesis is the variable being manipulated."
  - **Patterns in Agent's Response:**
    - The agent provides a final answer that reverses the roles of the independent and dependent variables.
    - The agent's explanation focuses on the variable being measured as the one being changed to achieve a desired outcome.
    - The agent does not clearly differentiate between the hypothesis and the variables in the experiment.
    - The agent jumps to conclusions without asking for clarification or further details about the task.

### Template Summary
This template is designed to identify errors where an agent misinterprets the roles of variables in an experimental setup. Key indicators include the agent's confusion between what is being manipulated and what is being measured, as well as a lack of clear distinction between the hypothesis and the variables. By asking specific questions and looking for consistent patterns in the agent's responses, similar errors can be detected early in the conversation.

### Error Schema for Identifying Similar Errors in Energy Concepts

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Misinterpretation of Energy Conversion**: The agent incorrectly interprets the conversion of energy, suggesting that potential energy increases when it should decrease, and vice versa.
- **Incorrect Application of Physical Laws**: The agent applies the law of gravitational potential energy (U = m g y) incorrectly, leading to a flawed conclusion about the direction of energy change.
- **Confusion in Sign Convention**: The agent uses a positive sign for an increase in energy and a negative sign for a decrease, but applies these conventions incorrectly in the context of the problem.
- **Overlooked Energy Conservation Principle**: The agent does not consider the principle of conservation of energy, which states that total energy remains constant in a closed system (neglecting non-conservative forces like friction).

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Task Type**: Questions involving basic physics principles, particularly energy conservation and energy conversion in a dynamic system (e.g., a sled descending a hill).
- **Agent Role**: The error often occurs in the initial analysis phase, typically handled by the Planner, before any data or file analysis.
- **Lack of Prior Information**: The error happens when the agent is not provided with or does not seek out additional information to validate the initial understanding or calculations.
- **Simplification of Physical Models**: The agent may simplify the physical model too much, neglecting important aspects such as the role of friction or air resistance, which can affect the energy changes.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions**:
  - "Can you explain the physical principles you used to determine the energy changes?"
  - "How do you account for the conversion of potential energy to kinetic energy in your analysis?"
  - "Did you consider the role of friction or air resistance in this scenario?"
  - "What would be the total energy of the system if we assume it is closed and no external forces are acting?"
- **Analytical Framework**:
  - **Check for Energy Conservation**: Verify if the agent's explanation aligns with the principle of conservation of energy.
  - **Evaluate Sign Conventions**: Assess whether the agent correctly applies the sign conventions for energy changes (positive for increase, negative for decrease).
  - **Review Application of Physical Laws**: Examine the application of physical laws, such as the formula for gravitational potential energy, to ensure they are used correctly.
  - **Consider All Forces**: Ensure that the agent has considered all relevant forces acting on the system, including conservative and non-conservative forces.
- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns**:
  - "Using U = m g y, the change in gravitational potential energy is ΔU = m g Δy"
  - "With both g and Δy negative, ΔU > 0, meaning potential energy increases"
  - "Therefore, the statement that best describes the energy changes is..."
  - "No prior specialist output to analyze" (indicating a lack of additional verification steps)
  - "Remember to: 1. Break down the task into manageable steps 2. Gather information systematically 3. Build upon previous results" (suggesting a procedural oversight)

This template is designed to help identify when an agent might be making similar errors in understanding and applying basic physical principles, particularly in scenarios involving energy changes in dynamic systems.

Generated Schema:
### Error Schema for Identifying Similar Errors in Multi-Agent Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Distinctive Patterns or Signals:**
  - **Repetition of General Information:** The agent repeatedly provides general information about the topic (e.g., location of Mount St. Helens, its classification as a stratovolcano) without addressing the specific question or task.
  - **Lack of Explicit Confirmation:** The agent fails to provide explicit, citable quotes or statements that directly answer the question (e.g., the geologic process responsible for the formation of Mount St. Helens).
  - **Inconsistent or Vague References:** The agent mentions related concepts (e.g., back-arc extension, rifting) but does not clearly link them to the core question, leading to potential confusion or misinterpretation.

- **Telltale Signs in Agent's Behavior or Responses:**
  - **Reopening the Same Page:** The agent reopens the same webpage multiple times without extracting the necessary information.
  - **Focusing on Unrelated Aspects:** The agent's responses focus on aspects of the task that are not directly relevant to the core question (e.g., feedback forms, page descriptions).
  - **Ignoring Specific Instructions:** The agent does not follow specific instructions to search for or extract information, instead providing a general overview or repeating metadata.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Contextual Conditions:**
  - **Complex Task with Specific Requirements:** The task involves a specific question that requires verification from authoritative sources and the extraction of precise, citable quotes.
  - **Multiple Sources Required:** The task involves gathering information from multiple sources (USGS and Britannica) to build a robust answer.
  - **Nuanced Information:** The task involves a topic with potential nuances (e.g., back-arc extension) that can lead to confusion if not clearly distinguished from the primary answer.

- **Sequence of Interactions:**
  - **Initial Breakdown and Source Selection:** The Planner correctly breaks down the task and selects the WebSurfer to gather information from authoritative sources.
  - **Repetitive Page Loading:** The WebSurfer repeatedly loads the same or similar pages without extracting the required specific information.
  - **General Information Overload:** The WebSurfer provides a lot of general information but fails to focus on the specific details needed to answer the question.
  - **Lack of Targeted Search:** The WebSurfer does not use the search/find function within the webpage to locate specific keywords or phrases related to the task.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions to Determine Error Presence:**
  - **Has the agent provided the exact quotes required?** 
    - "Did you find any explicit quotes from the USGS or Britannica that state Mount St. Helens lies at a subduction/convergent boundary, and the Juan de Fuca Plate subducts beneath the North American Plate?"
  - **Is the information relevant to the core question?**
    - "Is the information you provided directly addressing the geologic process that formed Mount St. Helens, or is it more general background information?"
  - **Have the specific instructions been followed?**
    - "Did you use the page search/find function to locate the keywords 'subduction', 'Juan de Fuca', and 'Cascade Range' on the USGS page?"

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Task-Specific Focus:** Evaluate whether the agent's responses are focused on the specific requirements of the task (e.g., exact quotes, precise citations).
  - **Relevance Check:** Assess the relevance of the information provided to the core question (e.g., does it directly answer the question about the geologic process).
  - **Instruction Compliance:** Verify if the agent has followed the detailed instructions given (e.g., using the search/find function, targeting specific sources).

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - **Repetition of General Information:**
    - "Mount St. Helens is a stratovolcano located in the Pacific Northwest region of the United States in the state of Washington."
  - **Lack of Explicit Confirmation:**
    - "The Cascade Range is a subduction zone, but no explicit quotes were found."
  - **Inconsistent or Vague References:**
    - "The Cascade Range is a subduction zone, and some sources mention back-arc extension or rifting."
  - **Ignoring Specific Instructions:**
    - "I loaded the USGS page, but did not search for the specific keywords."
  - **Metadata and Form Feedback:**
    - "The following metadata was extracted from the webpage," or "Was this page helpful?"

### Conclusion
This template helps identify when an agent is providing general information, failing to extract specific details, or not following targeted instructions, which can lead to errors in answering complex, specific questions in multi-agent conversations.

### Error Schema for Identifying Relationship Misinterpretation Errors in Multi-Agent Conversations

### Error Schema for Identifying Similar Errors in Multi-Agent Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:

  - **Misinterpretation of Cues:** The agent misinterprets the movement cues of organisms, such as conflating phototaxis with chemotaxis or other forms of taxis.
  - **Overgeneralization:** The agent generalizes the behavior of one organism to another without sufficient evidence or comparison, especially when the organisms belong to different taxonomic groups.

- **Telltale Signs in Agent's Behavior or Responses:**
  - **Rushed Analysis:** The agent quickly moves to a conclusion without thoroughly exploring all relevant aspects of the question.
  - **Lack of Verification:** The agent does not seek to verify or cross-check information from multiple credible sources.
  - **Inadequate Source Selection:** The agent relies on sources that are not authoritative or do not provide comprehensive information (e.g., search engine summaries instead of detailed academic articles).
  - **Unresolved Contradictions:** The agent presents contradictory information but does not address or resolve these contradictions in the final analysis.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Contextual Conditions:**
  - **Complex Biological Comparisons:** The conversation involves comparing biological traits or behaviors of different organisms, which can be nuanced and require detailed, specific information.
  - **Multiple Choice Questions:** The conversation is centered around a multiple-choice question, where the agent must select the best answer from a set of options, potentially leading to over-simplification.
  - **Limited Initial Information:** The initial information provided to the agent is limited or vague, requiring significant research to answer the question accurately.

- **Sequence of Interactions:**
  - **Initial Hypothesis Formation:** The agent forms an initial hypothesis based on limited or surface-level information.
  - **Sequential Information Gathering:** The agent gathers information sequentially, often focusing on one aspect of the question at a time without integrating all findings.
  - **Premature Conclusion:** The agent reaches a conclusion before all necessary information is gathered or verified.
  - **Lack of Comparative Analysis:** The agent fails to perform a comparative analysis of the behaviors or traits of the organisms in question, leading to an incorrect or incomplete answer.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions to Determine Error Presence:**
  - "Have you confirmed the movement mechanisms of both organisms?"
  - "Is there any conflicting information about the direction or nature of the movement of the organisms?"
  - "Can you verify if the movement toward an energy source is consistent for both organisms?"
  - "What is the evidence for the movement behavior of each organism?"

- **Analytical Framework:**

  - "However, another source mentions..."
  - "Before we conclude, we should verify..."
  - "Let's gather more information to compare..."
  - "It's important to note that both organisms..."

  - Lack of step-by-step reasoning or explanation of the underlying physics concepts.

  - Failure to differentiate between the forces acting on the object in the vertical and horizontal directions.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Typical Contextual Conditions:**
  - Tasks involving the application of Newton's laws of motion.
  - Questions about the forces acting on an object and the reasons for its motion.
  - Scenarios where multiple forces (e.g., gravity, normal force, friction) are at play.
  - Situations requiring the agent to explain the transition from static to kinetic friction.

- **Sequence of Interactions:**
  - The task is presented with a clear question and multiple-choice options.
  - The agent responds with a conclusion that is based on a partial understanding of the physical scenario.
  - The response is given without requesting additional information or capabilities (e.g., WebSurfer, FileSurfer) that could aid in a more accurate analysis.
  - The agent's decision-making process is not transparent, lacking a detailed breakdown of the forces involved.

  - "What force is causing the object to start moving?"

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Force Analysis:** Check if the agent correctly identifies and differentiates between all forces acting on the object (gravity, normal force, static and kinetic friction).
  - **Equilibrium vs. Motion:** Assess whether the agent understands that motion requires an unbalanced force and that equilibrium means no net force, leading to no acceleration.
  - **Threshold of Motion:** Evaluate if the agent recognizes the specific condition (Fs,max = μs N) that must be met for an object to overcome static friction and start moving.
  - **Step-by-Step Reasoning:** Look for a logical progression in the agent's reasoning, starting from the initial state of the object to the point of motion.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - "Gravity exerts a balanced force on the object."
  - "The forces acting on the object are in equilibrium."
  - "The object starts to move because the forces are balanced."
  - "No net force is acting on the object, so it starts to move."
  - "Once motion begins, the forces quickly balance out."
  - "The object moves due to equilibrium conditions."

### Error Schema for Identifying Misinterpretation of Independent Variables in Experimental Design

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Distinctive Patterns or Signals:**
  - The agent incorrectly identifies the independent variable by focusing on a factor that is kept constant across all experimental groups.

  - Overemphasis on variables that are mentioned as being the same across all groups.
  - Lack of specific reference to the variable that is being changed to observe its effect.
  - Incorrectly stating that a variable is the independent one when it is actually a controlled variable.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Contextual Conditions:**
  - The task involves an experiment with multiple variables (independent, dependent, and controlled).
  - The experiment description includes steps that are designed to control certain variables to ensure a fair test.
  - The question specifically asks for the independent variable, which is the variable being manipulated.

- **Sequence of Interactions:**
  - The user presents a clear experimental design with a list of steps and a question about the independent variable.
  - The agent does not ask clarifying questions about the experiment's design or the roles of the variables.
  - The agent provides a response that seems to align with a superficial reading of the task, ignoring the deeper structure of the experiment.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions to Determine if the Error is Present:**
  - "Can you explain which variable is being changed to observe its effect on the corn plants?"
  - "Which variable is the same across all groups, and why is it important to keep it constant?"
  - "What is the purpose of the independent variable in this experiment?"
  - "How do you distinguish between the independent variable and the controlled variables in this setup?"

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Step-by-Step Breakdown:** Evaluate if the agent has performed a step-by-step analysis of the experiment, identifying each variable's role.
  - **Variable Role Clarity:** Check if the agent clearly distinguishes between independent, dependent, and controlled variables.
  - **Manipulation Focus:** Assess whether the agent focuses on the variable that is being manipulated to observe its effect.
  - **Consistency Check:** Verify if the agent's final answer is consistent with the variable they identified as being manipulated.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - "All other conditions are kept constant" followed by an incorrect identification of the independent variable.
  - "The independent variable is [a controlled variable]" or similar statements.
  - Responses that do not explicitly state which variable is being changed to observe its effect.
  - Answers that seem to be based on a cursory reading of the task, lacking in-depth reasoning.

This template is designed to help identify when an agent misinterprets the independent variable in an experimental design task, focusing on the patterns and signals that indicate such an error is occurring.

### Error Schema for Identifying Confusion Between Earth's Rotation and Orbital Period in Multi-Agent Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Distinctive Patterns or Signals:**
  - **Incorrect Period Values:** The agent provides a rotation period of 365 days or any value close to it, which is the orbital period of Earth around the Sun.
  - **Misattribution of Periods:** The agent attributes the orbital period (365 days) to the rotation period, often in the context of a multiple-choice question or when asked to define a "day" on Earth.
  - **Mixed Definitions:** The agent combines or confuses the definitions of mean solar day and sidereal day, leading to an incorrect statement about the rotation period.

- **Telltale Signs in Agent's Behavior or Responses:**
  - **Repetition of Orbital Period:** The agent repeatedly mentions or returns the 365-day period in the context of Earth's rotation, even after being corrected.
  - **Lack of Clarity in Definitions:** The agent does not clearly distinguish between mean solar day and sidereal day, or between rotation and orbital periods.
  - **Inconsistent Information:** The agent provides correct information about the mean solar day and sidereal day but also includes an incorrect orbital period, leading to confusion.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Contextual Conditions:**
  - **Task Involves Multiple-Choice Questions:** The task is a multiple-choice question where the correct answer is the mean solar day (24 hours) and other options include the sidereal day (23 hours 56 minutes) and the orbital period (365 days).
  - **Sources Mentioned:** The conversation includes references to authoritative sources like NASA, Britannica, and MIT, but the agent may still pull information from less reliable or misinterpreted sources.
  - **Verification Steps:** The agent has been instructed to verify and distinguish between mean solar day and sidereal day, but the error persists, suggesting a misunderstanding or misreading of the sources.

- **Sequence of Interactions:**
  - **Initial Query:** The user asks a question about Earth's rotation period, providing multiple-choice options.
  - **First Web Search:** The WebSurfer performs a web search and returns correct information about mean solar day and sidereal day but includes an incorrect orbital period.
  - **Correction by Planner:** The Planner notes the error and instructs the WebSurfer to verify the information using authoritative sources.
  - **Subsequent Web Searches:** The WebSurfer performs additional searches but continues to return the incorrect orbital period, indicating a persistent misunderstanding.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions to Determine Error:**
  - **Clarify Definitions:** "What is the difference between a mean solar day and a sidereal day?"
  - **Confirm Rotation Period:** "What is the exact rotation period of Earth in hours?"
  - **Check Orbital Period:** "What is the orbital period of Earth around the Sun in days?"

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Consistency Check:** Compare the agent's responses for consistency. If the agent mentions both 24 hours and 365 days in the context of Earth's rotation, it indicates a confusion.
  - **Source Verification:** Ensure that the agent's sources are explicitly cited and are from authoritative sources like NASA, Britannica, or MIT. If the source is not clear or reliable, the information may be suspect.
  - **Definition Validation:** Validate the definitions provided by the agent. If the agent does not clearly define the mean solar day and sidereal day, or if the definitions are mixed, it suggests a misunderstanding.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - **Rotation vs. Orbital Period:** Phrases like "Earth’s rotation period is 365 days" or "Earth rotates around the Sun in 365 days" indicate the error.
  - **Lack of Distinguishing Language:** Absence of phrases like "mean solar day" and "sidereal day" in the agent's response, or using them interchangeably, suggests a lack of clear distinction.
  - **Multiple-Choice Context:** The error often appears in the context of a multiple-choice question where the options include 24 hours, 23 hours 56 minutes, and 365 days.

### Example of Error Identification:

- **WebSurfer Response:** "I found that Earth's rotation period is about 365 days, with a mean solar day of 24 hours and a sidereal day of 23 hours 56 minutes."

### Error Schema for Identifying Similar Errors in Future Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Distinctive Patterns or Signals:**

  - **Misinterpretation of Hypothesis Definition:** The agent misinterprets a statement that is framed as a proven fact (using words like "proven" or "demonstrated") as a hypothesis, which should instead be a verifiable and testable prediction or explanation.
  - **Overlooking Specificity:** The agent favors a general statement over a specific, testable prediction when identifying a hypothesis.

- **Telltale Signs in Agent's Behavior or Responses:**
  - **Lack of Detailed Analysis:** The agent provides a brief or superficial analysis of the options, often missing key criteria or nuances.
  - **Immediate Task Completion:** The agent marks the task as complete and provides a final answer without engaging in a systematic evaluation process.
  - **Use of Definitive Language:** The agent uses definitive language (e.g., "This is a hypothesis") without supporting the claim with a clear rationale or comparison against other options.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Typical Contextual Conditions:**
  - **Task Involves Multiple Choices:** The task requires the evaluation of multiple statements or options to identify which one fits a specific criterion (e.g., a scientific hypothesis).
  - **Criterion is Misunderstood:** The task involves a criterion that is often confused with similar concepts (e.g., a fact, a theory, a general principle).
  - **Limited Initial Information:** The task is presented with minimal context, and the agent does not seek additional information or clarification before making a decision.

- **Sequence of Interactions Preceding the Error:**
  - **Direct Questioning:** The user directly asks which statement is a hypothesis without providing additional guidance or context.
  - **Agent's Initial Response:** The agent acknowledges the task and begins to analyze the options.
  - **Quick Evaluation:** The agent evaluates the options quickly, possibly due to overconfidence or lack of depth in understanding the concept.
  - **Final Decision:** The agent makes a final decision and marks the task as complete without revisiting or questioning the initial analysis.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions to Determine Error Presence:**
  - "Can you explain the difference between a scientific hypothesis and a proven fact?"
  - "Why do you consider the selected statement to be a hypothesis? Can it be tested and verified through an experiment?"
  - "Are there any other statements that could also be considered a hypothesis? Why or why not?"

- **Analytical Framework for Identifying Error Pattern:**
  - **Step-by-Step Evaluation:** Ensure the agent breaks down each statement to assess whether it meets the criteria of a hypothesis (testable, verifiable, predictive).
  - **Comparison of Options:** Require the agent to compare the specificity and testability of each statement against the others.
  - **Verification of Understanding:** Ask the agent to define a hypothesis and apply that definition to each statement before making a final selection.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns as Reliable Indicators:**
  - "This is a hypothesis because..."
  - "The statement is proven to be..."
  - "In general, this method will..."
  - "I am marking the task as complete because..."
  - "The answer is clear..."
  - "No need to check further..."

By recognizing these error signatures, contextual conditions, and key phrases, future conversations can be monitored more effectively to identify when a similar error might be occurring.

  - **Review Information Sources:** Determine if the agent has utilized available information sources (like WebSurfer or FileSurfer) to gather or verify necessary data.
  - **Cross-Check Calculation Logic:** Ensure the agent's calculation logic is consistent with the principles of the subject (in this case, physics) and the values used.

  - "No previous specialist result exists. Proceeding with direct calculation."
  - "Based on the values provided, the calculation is..."

  - "The final answer is [Y] without reference to initial task analysis or data verification steps."

### Error Schema for Identifying Similar Errors in Future Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:

  - The user presents a clear, specific biological question.
  - The Planner agent analyzes the question and provides a reasoned response.

  - No follow-up or verification steps are taken to ensure the accuracy of the final answer.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions to Determine if the Error is Present:**
  - Does the agent’s final answer directly address the primary biological impact of somatic cell mutations?
  - Can the agent clearly explain the difference between somatic and germline cell mutations and their respective impacts?
  - Does the agent’s reasoning introduce mechanisms that are not directly related to the question’s focus?
  - Is the agent’s selected answer one of the secondary or indirect effects rather than the primary effect?

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Step 1: Identify the Core Question Focus**
    - Analyze the question to identify the primary biological process or impact being asked about.
    - Ensure the agent’s response is aligned with this core focus.
  - **Step 2: Evaluate the Biological Accuracy of Each Option**
    - Cross-check each option against known biological facts to determine if it accurately describes the impact of somatic cell mutations.
    - Look for any logical inconsistencies or misinterpretations in the agent’s evaluation of the options.
  - **Step 3: Assess the Depth of Understanding**
    - Evaluate whether the agent’s explanation demonstrates a deep understanding of the biological concepts involved.
    - Check if the agent’s reasoning is based on a clear and correct distinction between somatic and germline cells.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - **Misinterpretation of Somatic vs. Germline Mutations:**
    - "Somatic mutations can affect gamete formation" (This is a secondary effect, not a primary one.)
    - "Somatic mutations are passed on to offspring" (This is incorrect, as somatic mutations are not inherited.)
  - **Overemphasis on Indirect Effects:**
    - "By disrupting functions of somatic support cells, somatic mutations can harm an organism."
    - "Somatic mutations can indirectly harm the organism by affecting processes outside the somatic cells."
  - **Lack of Direct Answer:**
    - "The correct answer is not explicitly mentioned in the explanation."
    - "The reasoning provided is correct, but the final answer is wrong."
  - **Confusion in Logical Flow:**
    - "While [option] is true, it is not the best answer because [reason]."
    - "Option [X] is more relevant because [secondary mechanism]."

### Error Schema for Identifying Predator-Prey Dynamics Misinterpretation Errors

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Distinctive Patterns or Signals:**
  - **Misapplication of General Principles:** The agent applies general ecological principles (e.g., predator removal increases prey population) without considering the specific context or behavior of the species involved.
  - **Overlooking Behavioral Responses:** The agent fails to account for the behavioral changes in prey populations that can occur due to the absence of predators, such as reduced risk-induced movements and activity.
  - **Incorrect Option Selection:** The agent selects an option that contradicts the specific ecological evidence or context provided in the task, such as choosing an increase in prey population when the evidence suggests a decrease due to behavioral changes.

- **Telltale Signs in the Agent's Behavior or Responses:**
  - **Lack of Specificity:** The agent's response is vague and does not reference specific studies or examples that directly relate to the species in question (e.g., hawks and rodents).
  - **Generalization Without Evidence:** The agent generalizes the ecological principle without providing concrete evidence or examples that support the specific scenario.
  - **Inconsistent Logic:** The agent's reasoning jumps from a general principle to a specific outcome without a logical bridge, often missing the intermediate steps or mechanisms that would lead to the outcome.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Contextual Conditions:**
  - **Task Involves Predator-Prey Relationships:** The task is centered around a scenario involving the interaction between predators and prey, often in a specific ecological or agricultural context.
  - **Multiple-Choice Options:** The task presents multiple-choice options, one of which is a direct application of a general ecological principle, while another requires a more nuanced understanding of the specific species' behaviors and interactions.
  - **Credible Sources Available:** There are credible sources available that discuss the specific roles and behaviors of the species involved, but the agent either does not find or does not correctly interpret these sources.

- **Sequence of Interactions:**
  - **Initial Generalization:** The agent initially responds with a general ecological principle, such as "removing a top predator can lead to an increase in prey populations."
  - **Lack of Specific Follow-Up:** The agent does not follow up with specific searches or references to studies that directly address the behavior and population dynamics of the species in question.
  - **Repetitive General Searches:** The agent may perform multiple general searches without narrowing down to the specific scenario or species, leading to a misinterpretation of the results.
  - **Final Incorrect Answer:** The agent concludes with an incorrect answer, often based on the general principle rather than the specific evidence gathered.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions to Determine Error Presence:**
  - **"What specific studies or examples support your answer?"**
  - **"How does the behavior of the prey species (e.g., rodents) change in the absence of the predator (e.g., hawks)?"**
  - **"Can you explain the intermediate steps or mechanisms that lead to the outcome you've chosen?"**

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Context-Specific Analysis:** Evaluate whether the agent's response is context-specific. Does it consider the unique behaviors and interactions of the species mentioned in the task?
  - **Behavioral Dynamics:** Check if the agent has considered the behavioral changes in prey populations that can occur due to the absence of predators, such as reduced fear responses and altered movement patterns.
  - **Intermediate Mechanisms:** Assess whether the agent has logically connected the general ecological principle to the specific outcome through intermediate mechanisms or steps.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - **General Principles Without Specific Evidence:** Phrases like "predator removal increases prey population," "trophic cascade," or "ecological balance" without specific references to the species or context.
  - **Lack of Nuance:** Responses that do not delve into the specific ecological roles of the species, such as "hawks regulate rodent populations" without explaining how or why.
  - **Over-Simplification:** Conversations that simplify the scenario too much, ignoring the complex interactions and behavioral responses of the prey species.
  - **Repetitive General Searches:** The agent repeatedly searches for general ecological principles or predator-prey dynamics without focusing on the specific species or scenario.

### Example of Application:
- **Initial Response:**
  - Agent: "If hawks are removed, the rodent population would increase because removing a top predator can lead to a trophic cascade effect."
  - **Detection:** This response is a generalization without specific evidence. Ask: "What specific studies or examples support your answer?"

- **Follow-Up Response:**
  - Agent: "Hawks are known to regulate rodent populations, and removing them would lead to an increase in rodent numbers."
  - **Detection:** The agent is still lacking nuance and specific evidence. Ask: "

  - **Weight Justification:** Assess whether the agent provides a clear and contextually appropriate explanation for the weights assigned to different criteria.
  - **Comprehensive Evaluation:** Determine if the agent has systematically evaluated all provided options against the full range of the subject's functions.

  - "The closest functional match is..."
  - "The stem provides structural support, similar to how an anchor holds..."
  - "Using a weighted criterion that prioritizes..."
  - "Final Answer: [Option] because it [matches one function]..."
  - Lack of phrases indicating a multi-step reasoning process, such as "Let's analyze each option in turn," "Considering the transport function," or "Evaluating the role of support in each analogy."

This template can be used to quickly identify when an agent is making a decision based on a limited or biased understanding of the subject's functions, which can lead to incorrect analogies in multiple-choice questions.

Generated Schema:
### Error Schema for Identifying Similar Errors in Future Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Distinctive Patterns or Signals:**
  - **Repetition of Information:** The agent repeats the same information multiple times without advancing the conversation or providing new insights.
  - **Lack of Specific Content:** The agent provides metadata or general descriptions of sources without extracting specific, relevant content or quotes.
  - **Inconsistent Progression:** The agent's responses show a lack of clear progression towards the goal, often revisiting the same steps or sources without moving forward.

- **Telltale Signs in Agent's Behavior or Responses:**
  - **Verbose Descriptions:** The agent provides detailed descriptions of the webpage layout and metadata instead of focusing on the relevant content.
  - **Incomplete Information:** The agent fails to provide the required components (URL, verbatim quotes, layperson takeaways) for each source, often stopping at the abstract or initial page load.
  - **Redundant Actions:** The agent repeatedly opens the same webpage or source without extracting the necessary information, indicating a lack of understanding of the task requirements.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Contextual Conditions:**
  - **Initial Task Complexity:** The task involves gathering specific information from multiple sources, which requires the agent to navigate and extract content systematically.
  - **Requirement for Verbatim Quotes:** The task explicitly requires verbatim quotes from sources, which the agent often fails to provide, instead offering paraphrased or incomplete information.
  - **Need for Layperson-Friendly Takeaways:** The task requires the agent to provide simple, understandable takeaways for each quote, a step that is often overlooked or poorly executed.

- **Sequence of Interactions:**
  - **Initial Request for Quotes:** The user or planner requests specific quotes from multiple sources to support a particular answer.
  - **Agent Opens Sources:** The agent opens the requested sources but provides metadata or general descriptions instead of the specific quotes.
  - **Repetition of Instructions:** The planner repeats the instructions, emphasizing the need for verbatim quotes and layperson-friendly takeaways, but the agent continues to provide incomplete or redundant information.
  - **Stagnation in Task Progress:** The conversation stalls as the agent fails to advance beyond the initial steps of opening sources, despite repeated guidance.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions:**
  - **Quote Verification:** "Can you confirm if the verbatim quotes you provided explicitly connect lever-press learning to behavior modification based on past reinforcement?"
  - **Content Extraction:** "Did you extract the specific content from the full text or are you relying on the abstract?"
  - **Progress Check:** "Can you summarize the key information you have gathered so far and how it supports the answer we are looking for?"

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Content Relevance:** Evaluate if the agent's responses are directly relevant to the task requirements, specifically the need for verbatim quotes and layperson-friendly takeaways.
  - **Progression Monitoring:** Track the sequence of the agent's actions to ensure they are moving forward and not repeating the same steps.
  - **Completeness Assessment:** Check if the agent has provided all the required components for each source (URL, verbatim quotes, layperson takeaways).

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - **Repetition of General Information:** "During operant conditioning, animals associate actions with outcomes" (repeated without context or specific quotes).
  - **Focus on Metadata:** "The following metadata was extracted from the webpage:" (used excessively without providing the requested content).
  - **Incomplete Source Information:** "The viewport shows X% of the webpage" (indicating the agent is not fully opening or reading the source).
  - **Lack of Action:** "The web browser is open to the page [URL]" (repeated without further action or content extraction).
  - **Redundant Steps:** "I typed [URL] into the browser address bar" (repeated multiple times without advancing the task).

### Example of Error Detection:
- **User/Planner Input:** "Please extract 1–2 sentence verbatim quotes from the full text of the article that explicitly connect lever-press learning to behavior modification based on past reinforcement, along with a layperson-friendly takeaway for each quote."
- **Agent Response:** "The web browser is open to the page [Task parameters influence operant response variability in mice - PMC](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9894580/). The viewport shows 3% of the webpage, and is positioned at the top of the page. The following metadata was extracted from the webpage: [...]"
- **Detection:** The agent's response is focused on the metadata and initial webpage load, without providing the required verbatim quotes or takeaways. This indicates a repetition of general information and an incomplete content extraction, which are key error signatures.

Generated Schema:
### Error Schema for Identifying Similar Errors in Future Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Misalignment with Ground Truth:** The agent selects or proposes an answer that does not align with the ground truth, even when the correct information has been repeatedly mentioned or confirmed.
- **Confusion in Terminology:** The agent misinterprets or misuses key terms or concepts that are crucial to the correct understanding of the topic.
- **Premature Conclusion:** The agent concludes the task or provides a final answer before gathering sufficient evidence or confirming the correct information from multiple sources.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Contextual Conditions:**
  - The task involves a multiple-choice question where the correct answer is explicitly supported by available information.
  - The agent has access to credible sources that clearly state the correct answer.
  - The agent has been given multiple instructions to verify and corroborate the information from different sources.
- **Sequence of Interactions:**
  - Initial analysis correctly identifies the key process (e.g., lithification involving compaction and cementation).
  - Subsequent searches or interactions with specialists provide additional support for the correct answer.
  - Despite this, the agent either ignores or misinterprets the correct information and proposes a different answer (e.g., choosing option C instead of B).

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions to Determine Error Presence:**
  - **Question 1:** "Can you confirm which option is supported by the majority of the sources you have consulted?"
  - **Question 2:** "What evidence from the sources do you have that supports your final answer?"
  - **Question 3:** "Are there any discrepancies between the sources you found and the options provided in the question?"
- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Step 1:** Review the agent's initial analysis to ensure it aligns with the correct understanding of the topic.
  - **Step 2:** Evaluate the agent's responses to subsequent searches or specialist engagements to check for consistency in the information gathered.
  - **Step 3:** Compare the final answer with the ground truth and the evidence provided by the agent.
  - **Step 4:** Assess if the agent has ignored or misinterpreted any clear and repeated evidence from credible sources.
- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - **Phrase 1:** "This supports option [incorrect option]" when the correct option has already been supported by multiple sources.
  - **Phrase 2:** "The correct choice is [incorrect option]" after a series of steps that should have led to the correct answer.
  - **Pattern 1:** The agent provides a final answer that contradicts the information gathered in earlier steps without explaining the discrepancy.
  - **Pattern 2:** The agent selects an option that is partially correct but not the most comprehensive or accurate, based on the available evidence.

### Example Application of the Template:
- **Initial Analysis:** The Planner correctly identified that sedimentary rocks form through deposition followed by lithification, which includes compaction and cementation.
- **Subsequent Searches:** The WebSurfer provided multiple sources (Britannica, USGS, LibreTexts) that consistently supported the lithification process involving compaction and cementation.
- **Final Answer:** The Planner concluded with option C (Chemical reactions change minerals) instead of option B (Materials are pressed together), despite the evidence supporting option B.
- **Detection:**
  - **Question 1:** "Can you confirm which option is supported by the majority of the sources you have consulted?" (Expected response: Option B)
  - **Question 2:** "What evidence from the sources do you have that supports your final answer?" (Expected response: Evidence should point to compaction and cementation, not chemical reactions alone)
  - **Step 1:** The initial analysis correctly identified compaction and cementation.
  - **Step 2:** The subsequent searches consistently supported this understanding.
  - **Step 3:** The final answer (C) does not align with the ground truth (B).
  - **Step 4:** The Planner ignored the repeated evidence from credible sources that supported option B.
- **Key Phrases:**
  - "This supports option C" (indicating a misinterpretation or confusion)
  - "The correct choice is C" (premature conclusion or misalignment with evidence)

=== Schema for Error Log 203 ===
Generated Schema:
### Error Schema for Identifying Similar Errors in Future Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Distinctive Patterns or Signals:**
  - **Incorrect Final Answer:** The agent provides a final answer that is factually incorrect.
  - **Premature Task Completion:** The agent declares the task complete without engaging any of the available specialists or performing a systematic check.
  - **Misinterpretation of Universal Features:** The agent incorrectly states that a cellular structure is universal to all cells of a certain type when it is not (e.g., stating that all animal cells have mitochondria).
  - **Omission of Key Structures:** The agent omits key structures that are indeed common to both plant and animal cells.
  - **Inclusion of Non-Common Structures:** The agent includes structures that are not common to both types of cells in the final answer.

- **Telltale Signs in the Agent's Behavior or Responses:**
  - **Lack of Verification Steps:** The agent does not break down the task into steps or mention using any specialists to verify the information.
  - **Confusion of Equivalent Structures:** The agent conflates or incorrectly equates structures (e.g., considering the animal extracellular matrix as equivalent to a cell wall).
  - **Overgeneralization:** The agent makes broad, generalized statements about cellular components without considering exceptions or variations.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Contextual Conditions:**
  - **Type of Task:** The task involves identifying common or universal features between two distinct biological entities (in this case, plant and animal cells).
  - **Knowledge Domain:** The task is within the domain of biology, specifically cellular biology.
  - **Multiple Choice Format:** The task is presented in a multiple-choice format, which can sometimes lead to oversimplified or incorrect responses.

- **Sequence of Interactions:**
  - **Initial Request:** The user provides a clear, specific question that can be answered with factual knowledge.
  - **Planner's Immediate Response:** The Planner responds immediately with a final answer, bypassing the step of breaking down the task or engaging specialists.
  - **No Follow-Up Actions:** The Planner does not suggest or take any follow-up actions to verify the accuracy of the response.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions to Determine Presence of Error:**
  - "Are all the structures you mentioned common to both plant and animal cells?"
  - "Can you provide an example of an animal cell that does not contain one of the structures you listed?"
  - "How did you verify that these structures are indeed common to both plant and animal cells?"
  - "Did you consider the variations in cell types when determining your answer?"

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Step-by-Step Verification:** Check if the agent has broken down the task into smaller steps and verified the information using available specialists or resources.
  - **Cross-Referencing:** Ensure the agent has cross-referenced the answer with known biological facts or data.
  - **Exception Consideration:** Evaluate if the agent has considered exceptions or variations in cell types (e.g., specialized cells in animals or plants that might lack certain structures).

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - **"No prior specialist output yet"** followed by a final answer, indicating a lack of systematic information gathering.
  - **"This is a straightforward [subject] fact-check"** without subsequent steps to verify the facts.
  - **"TASK_COMPLETE"** at the beginning of the response, suggesting the agent has not engaged in a thorough process.
  - **Generalizations like "all [type] cells have [structure]"** without qualification or mention of exceptions.
  - **Incorrect equivalences, e.g., "cell wall (including the animal extracellular matrix)"** without proper justification or context.

By using this template, future errors of a similar nature can be more easily identified, ensuring that the conversation analysis remains robust and accurate.

Generated Schema:
### Error Schema for Identifying Similar Errors in Future Conversations

#### 1. Error Signatures:
- **Distinctive Patterns or Signals:**
  - The agent provides an incorrect final answer to a question that has a clear, factual correct answer.
  - The agent demonstrates a misunderstanding of fundamental scientific concepts, particularly in chemistry or physics.
  - The agent conflates the properties of subatomic particles with the properties of the atom or element as a whole.
  - The agent jumps to a conclusion without breaking down the task or gathering necessary information, leading to a logically flawed but seemingly plausible answer.
  - The agent incorrectly identifies the role of different components within an atom in determining the element's characteristics.

- **Telltale Signs in Agent's Behavior or Responses:**
  - The agent's response includes a detailed explanation that is incorrect but sounds authoritative.
  - The agent does not use any of the available capabilities (WebSurfer, FileSurfer, Coder, CodeExecutor) to verify or research the answer.
  - The agent's decision-making process is abbreviated, often concluding the task with minimal analysis.
  - The agent shows confidence in an answer that contradicts basic scientific knowledge.

#### 2. Error Context Analysis:
- **Contextual Conditions:**
  - The task involves a multiple-choice question where the options are closely related scientific terms (e.g., atom, electron, nucleus, proton).
  - The task requires an understanding of the structure of atoms and the properties that define elements.
  - The task is presented in a straightforward manner, without complex or ambiguous phrasing.
  - The agent is expected to engage in a step-by-step analysis but fails to do so, possibly due to overconfidence or lack of domain-specific knowledge.

- **Sequence of Interactions:**
  - The user poses a question that has a clear scientific answer.
  - The Planner analyzes the task without breaking it down into smaller, verifiable steps.
  - The Planner provides a final answer based on a flawed understanding of the scientific concept.
  - The Planner marks the task as complete without using any additional resources or capabilities to double-check the information.

#### 3. Detection Heuristics:
- **Specific Questions to Determine Error Presence:**
  - "Can you explain how you arrived at this conclusion?"
  - "What is the role of electrons in determining the characteristics of an element?"
  - "How does the atomic structure of copper differ from that of other elements?"
  - "Can you identify the smallest unit of any element that retains its defining properties?"
  - "Did you consider using any of the available capabilities to verify your answer?"

- **Analytical Framework:**
  - **Concept Verification:** Check if the agent's understanding of the basic scientific concepts (e.g., atomic structure, subatomic particles, element characteristics) is accurate.
  - **Step-by-Step Analysis:** Evaluate whether the agent has broken down the task into logical, manageable steps and used these steps to arrive at a conclusion.
  - **Use of Resources:** Determine if the agent has utilized any of the available capabilities (WebSurfer, FileSurfer, Coder, CodeExecutor) to gather or verify information.
  - **Confidence Level:** Assess the level of confidence in the agent's response and whether it is justified by the depth of analysis and verification.

- **Key Phrases or Conversation Patterns:**
  - Phrases indicating overconfidence: "The answer is clearly...", "There's no doubt that...", "It's obvious that..."
  - Phrases showing a lack of step-by-step reasoning: "I think the answer is...", "Based on my understanding...", "I conclude that..."
  - Phrases that conflate subatomic particles: "The element’s identity is fixed by its atomic number (the number of protons) housed in the nucleus; electrons influence chemical reactivity but do not determine what element it is."
  - Immediate task completion without further steps: "TASK_COMPLETE" followed by a final answer without any intermediate actions or verifications.
